# Consideration of funding proposals – Addendum VII Funding proposal package for FP197

## Summary

This addendum contains the following six parts:

a) A funding proposal summary titled "Green Guarantee Company ("GGC")" submitted by MUFG Bank Ltd;

b) No-rejection letter issued by the national designated authority or focal point(s);

c) Environmental and social assessment report;

d) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;

e) Response of the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment; and

f) Gender documentation of the funding proposal.

These documents are provided directly from the accredited entity and the national designated authority[s] or focal point[s], respectively. Pursuant to the Comprehensive Information Disclosure Policy of the Fund, the funding proposal titled "Green Guarantee Company ("GGC")" by MUFG Bank Ltd is subject to redaction in accordance with the policy. The Fund has taken steps to ensure confidentiality of certain proprietary, legally privileged or commercially sensitive information of the entity.

GCF/B.34/02/Add.07/Rev.01
16 October 2022

## Note on Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template
- Amendments to the funding proposal template must be made in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.
- Any amendments to the funding proposal template must be consistent with and reflect the details provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in all relevant documents.
- In the event that a funding proposal is submitted with an amendment exceeding 10% of the original length of the proposal, exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.
- The revised proposal will be subject to the same review process as the original proposal.

Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website. Proposals that are not considered for funding may also be disclosed in accordance with the IDP. Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section 14A.

fundingproposal@gcfund.org

## A.21. Executive summary (max 750 words, approximately 1 page)

### The Climate Change Problem

- **At the 2008 Climate Summit (COP13) in Copenhagen**, developed countries made a commitment to increase climate finance for developing countries (defined for the purposes of this document as countries that are eligible for Overseas Development Assistance).
- **It was generally agreed** that the COP15 target to provide USD 100 billion of climate finance a year by 2020 has been missed. In October, the UN COP presidency following it will be met by 2023, but even this delayed milestone would be insufficient.


---
**Page 7**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V9.0 | PAGE 3

There is a continued need for additional international financial support requested by developing countries to shift their country-specific greenhouse gases (GHG) savings targets and to move more resilient pathways based on their country’s vulnerability assessment.

1.3 One of the most prominent financial innovations and results in the area of climate finance over the past ten years has been the unprecedented and rapid growth of the global green bond market, as exemplified by the green bond market, demonstrated in Figure 1 below:

| Figure 1: Global Green Bond Issuance, USD billion |
| --- |
| Developed Market Green Bonds | Emerging Market Green Bonds | Supranational green bonds |

| 2013 | 2014 | 2015 | 2016 | 2017 | 2018 | 2019 | 2020 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0.3 | 0.4 | 0.5 | 0.7 | 1.0 | 1.6 | 2.8 | 4.0 |

1.4 International Capital Market Association (ICMA) defines a "green bond" or loan as any type of bond or loan instrument where the proceeds will be exclusively applied to finance new or existing eligible projects, property, or assets.

1.5 Despite the unprecedented challenges for the global economy and financial markets in 2020, the global green bond market proved resilient, achieving key milestone at USD 1 trillion in cumulative issuance since 2007, with usage of USD 34 billion in 2020 alone. The total volume of green bonds issued in 2020 was 58% higher than that recorded in the previous year.

Proposed Interventions: Guarantees as a Solution

1.6 The OECD defines guarantees as "a type of insurance policy protecting banks and investors from the risks of non-payment". More technically, a guarantee is a legally binding agreement under which the guarantor agrees to pay part or all amount due on an obligation if the obligor fails to meet its obligations in full and on time. This can be used by governments to reduce the risk of investment.

1.7 According to OECD data on amounts mobilised from the private sector by official development finance interventions between 2012 and 2018, guarantees mobilised more private capital than direct lending or equity investments. The amount of funds mobilised through guarantees was USD 35 billion in 2018 compared with USD 29 billion for direct lending and USD 24 billion for equity investments. This represents a compound annual growth rate of 14%. Guarantees have mobilised more private finance than any other financial instrument, representing an important role in the development of green bonds.

This level of market transformation is not possible without guarantees. The OECD data shows that this is true even when there are no hard currency guarantees focused on global capital markets. This is because guarantees help to address the information asymmetry (i.e., investor perception of risk is actually greater than the actual level of risk) and also help to reduce transaction costs, which in turn can lead to lower interest rates for green bonds. In addition, they can help further transactions without credit enhancement once guarantees build up the capacity of investors to the point that they are able to invest in green bonds from developing countries without any credit enhancement.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V9.0 | PAGE 4

## Climate Results / Benefits

### GGC believes that guarantees can help developing countries access more stable finance from the global green financing market and to be able to attract, mobilize and deploy funds to build, operate and maintain Green Climate Projects (‘GGCPs’) as a key in a market champion and mobilize greater private sector climate finance into developing countries. GGC will seek to guarantee global investors to catalyse their investments into green bonds and green loans that are issued by developing countries in global credit and capital markets. GGC will also seek to ensure that the GGCPs are listed on major exchanges, such as the London Stock Exchange (‘LSE’), which is a global market for capital raising and investment grade rating has been obtained from at least one of these three major rating agencies.

### To ensure speed of implementation the GGC proposal is based on working with existing global credit and capital markets infrastructure. The GGCPs will be listed on the LSE, which provides access to a large number of institutional investors who have the opportunity to scale up speed. Initial legal advice has confirmed that there are no legal or regulatory obstacles to GGC being recognized as a market champion in the UK and elsewhere.

### GGC’s green bonds and loans from and by developing countries listed on the London Stock Exchange (‘LSE’) will attract global credit and capital markets investors, which will help to mobilize greater private sector climate finance into developing countries. The GGCPs are expected to be listed on the LSE in a manner that is consistent with the listing of municipal bonds and allowing an ecosystem to mobilise greater climate finance into developing countries.

### GGC’s Guarantee Policy (see Annex 23) will focus on guaranteeing green bonds and loans which enable developing countries to meet their climate adaptation and mitigation targets.

## TRANSACTION SELECTION PROCESS

### Figure 3: Transaction Selection Process

| Step | Description |
| --- | --- |
| **STEP 1** | **TRANSACTION ORIGINATION**<br>• Financial analysis<br>• Training of origination partners |
| **STEP 2** | **INITIAL SCIENCE SCREENING**<br>• Initial screening to determine if project meets GCF’s Transaction Screener (Annex 24B) and is eligible for further consideration. The initial screening will be conducted by the GCF Impact Specialist. |
| **STEP 3** | **REVIEW & CHALLENGE**<br>• Following a professional review of the available information and documents, the GCF Impact Committee will make a recommendation to the GCF Board on whether or not the project should proceed to the next stage in the TSP process. The GCF Board will be voting members of the GCF Impact Committee. As set out above, three members in each of the two regions will be appointed by the GCF Executive Director. The GCF Impact Committee will make a recommendation to the GCF Impact Committee to ensure there has been sufficient rigor in the various assessment methods employed and that the project meets all relevant criteria for consideration under the TSP process. |
| **STEP 4** | **DEEP DIVE DUE DILIGENCE**<br>• The due diligence begins with GCF engaging an independent in-country consultant to undertake a site visit with GCF’s Impact Team and the borrower. In parallel with a physical site visit, they will also review all relevant documents provided by the borrower. This process is designed to communicate with aspirations and claims made in the documents. The outcome of the due diligence step will be an assessment of whether or not there are any material deviations from the project’s original design that provide evidence to validate the various transaction assessments, analyses, and their underlying assumptions. Critically, it also provides a basis for identifying meaningful deviations that have been uncovered during due diligence which will need to be addressed by the borrower. |
| **STEP 5** | **APPROVAL**<br>• The TSP process is concluded with a final review of the transaction by the GCF Impact Committee and Civil Approval. |

### Step 1: Transaction Origination (1-2 weeks): Transactions will be originated by GCF’s Origination Partners. GCF’s Origination Partners have been tasked with project transaction’s borrowers to gather the latest information and documents for the proposed transaction.

### Step 2: Initial Screening (3-5 weeks): Once an Origination Partner has introduced a transaction to GCF, or the Manager of GCF, then GCF Impact Team will undertake an initial screening using the screening tools on the Transaction Selection Screener.

### Step 3: Review and Challenge (1-3 weeks): Following a professional review of the available information and documents, the GCF Impact Committee will make a recommendation to the GCF Board on whether or not the project should proceed to the next stage in the TSP process. The GCF Board will be voting members of the GCF Impact Committee.

### Step 4: Deep Dive Due Diligence (2-6 weeks): The due diligence begins with GCF engaging an independent in-country consultant to undertake a site visit with GCF’s Impact Team and the borrower. In parallel with a physical site visit, they will also review all relevant documents provided by the borrower.

### Step 5: Approval (1 week): The TSP process is concluded with a final review of the transaction by the GCF Impact Committee and Civil Approval.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V9.0 | PAGE 7

#### Step 2: Remediation & Disclosure (4-5 weeks): Where gaps and/or deviations exist, the transaction will be rejected and the borrower will be required to develop a remediation strategy that satisfactorily addresses the identified gaps/deviations. This process is managed by the GGC Impact Team. The borrower must provide evidence of progress in developing the remediation strategy once it has been developed. Once the remediation strategy has been developed, the borrower is required to commit to delivering and implementing this strategy within a reasonable timeframe (typically 12 months). If there is any reluctance to developing and/or delivering a remediation strategy, the transaction will be rejected by the GGC Impact Team.

#### Step 3: Monitoring & Evaluation Strategy (2-4 weeks): The M&E Strategy must receive a clear commitment by the borrower before it is tabled alongside the Transaction Scoring (Annex 2A), the detailed transaction DD report, and the remediation strategy. If the borrower does not provide this commitment or if there are any issues with the M&E Strategy, the transaction will be rejected by the GGC Investment Team before it is tabled with GGC Impact Committee for consideration.

#### Step 4: Approval (1-2 weeks): The GGC Impact Committee, which comprises independent experts (see Section 1.26), will review and approve or reject transactions based on the information provided in Annexes 2A & B, the remediation strategy provided by the GGC Impact Team. Once satisfied that the transaction is compliant with GGC’s Frameworks, it will be tabled alongside the Transaction Scoring (Annex 2A) and the M&E Strategy for consideration at the GGC Credit Committee meeting.

#### Step 5: Remediation & Disclosure (4-5 weeks): If a transaction has been rejected by the GGC Impact Committee due to gaps or deviations, it will be returned to step 2. If a transaction is approved by the GGC Impact Committee and requires remediation, it will be returned to step 3.

#### Step 6: Monitoring & Evaluation Strategy (2-4 weeks): The M&E Strategy must receive a clear commitment from the borrower before it is tabled alongside the Transaction Scoring (Annex 2A), the detailed transaction DD report, and the remediation strategy. If the borrower does not provide this commitment or if there are any issues with the M&E Strategy, the transaction will be rejected by the GGC Investment Team before it is tabled with GGC Impact Committee for consideration.

#### Step 7: Approval (1-2 weeks): The GGC Impact Committee, which comprises independent experts (see Section 1.26), will review and approve or reject transactions based on the information provided in Annexes 2A & B, the remediation strategy provided by the GGC Impact Team. Once satisfied that the transaction is compliant with GGC’s Frameworks, it will be tabled alongside the Transaction Scoring (Annex 2A) and the M&E Strategy for consideration at the GGC Credit Committee meeting.

#### Step 8: Approval (1-2 weeks): If a transaction has been rejected by the GGC Impact Committee due to gaps or deviations, it will be returned to step 2. If a transaction is approved by the GGC Impact Committee and requires remediation, it will be returned to step 3. If a transaction is approved by the GGC Impact Committee but does not require remediation, it will progress to Step 9.

#### Step 9: Approval (1-2 weeks): The GGC Credit Committee, which comprises independent experts (see Section 1.26), will review and approve or reject transactions based on the information provided in Annexes 2A & B, the M&E Strategy provided by the GGC Impact Team, and any other relevant documents. If a transaction is approved by the GGC Credit Committee, it will be tabled alongside the Transaction Scoring (Annex 2A) for consideration at the GGC Board meeting.

#### Step 10: Approval (1-2 weeks): The GGC Board will review and approve or reject transactions based on the information provided in Annexes 2A & B. If a transaction is approved by the GGC Board, it will be tabled alongside the Transaction Scoring (Annex 2A) for consideration at the GGC Investment Committee meeting.

#### Step 11: Approval (1-2 weeks): The GGC Investment Committee will review and approve or reject transactions based on the information provided in Annexes 2A & B. If a transaction is approved by the GGC Investment Committee, it will be tabled alongside the Transaction Scoring (Annex 2A) for consideration at the GGC Board meeting.

#### Step 12: Approval (1-2 weeks): The GGC Board will review and approve or reject transactions based on the information provided in Annexes 2A & B. If a transaction is approved by the GGC Board, it will be tabled alongside the Transaction Scoring (Annex 2A) for consideration at the GGC Investment Committee meeting.

#### Step 13: Approval (1-2 weeks): The GGC Credit Committee will only approve transactions that have first been approved by the GGC Impact Committee. If a transaction is rejected by the GGC Impact Committee, it will be returned to step 5. If a transaction has been approved by both the GGC Impact and Credit Committees, it will progress to Step 14.

#### Step 14: Approval (1-2 weeks): The GGC Investment Committee will only approve transactions that have first been approved by the GGC Impact Committee. If a transaction is rejected by the GGC Impact Committee, it will be returned to step 5. If a transaction has been approved by both the GGC Impact and Credit Committees, it will progress to Step 15.

#### Step 15: Approval (1-2 weeks): The GGC Board will only approve transactions that have first been approved by the GGC Impact Committee. If a transaction is rejected by the GGC Impact Committee, it will be returned to step 5. If a transaction has been approved by both the GGC Impact and Credit Committees, it will progress to Step 16.

#### Step 16: Approval (1-2 weeks): The GGC Investment Committee will only approve transactions that have first been approved by the GGC Impact Committee. If a transaction is rejected by the GGC Impact Committee, it will be returned to step 5. If a transaction has been approved by both the GGC Impact and Credit Committees, it will progress to Step 17.

If a transaction is rejected by the GGC Impact Committee, it will be returned to step 5. If a transaction has been approved by both the GGC Impact and Credit Committees, it will progress to Step 22.

#### Step 22: Approval (1-2 weeks): The GGC Investment Committee will only approve transactions that have first been approved by the GGC Impact Committee. If a transaction is rejected by the GGC Impact Committee, it will be returned to step 5. If a transaction has been approved by both the GGC Impact and Credit Committees, it will progress to Step 23.

#### Step 23: Approval (1-2 weeks): The GGC Board will only approve transactions that have first been approved by the GGC Impact Committee. If a transaction is rejected by the GGC Impact Committee, it will be returned to step 5. If a transaction has been approved by both the GGC Impact and Credit Committees, it will progress to Step 24.

#### Step 24: Approval (1-2 weeks): The GGC Investment Committee will only approve transactions that have first been approved by the GGC Impact Committee. If a transaction is rejected by the GGC Impact Committee, it will be returned to step 5. If a transaction has been approved by both the GGC Impact and Credit Committees, it will progress to Step 25.

#### Step 25: Approval (1-2 weeks): The GGC Board will only approve

# B. PROJECT PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## 8.1 Climate Change (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### Climate Change Problem

The climate change problem GGC addresses is mobilising climate financing from developed countries to developing countries to support more mitigation and adaptation projects and programmes, particularly by way of private sector investment.

#### Baseline

3 Actions to limit or reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions intensity and to better prepare for and respond to climate change impacts are required. The costs associated with these actions vary depending on what the world needs to spend to meet the Paris Agreement's 1.5°C target and what the costs of adaptation may be. Meeting the USD 500 billion a year by 2030 needed to support developing member states. To date, however this has remained just under $400 billion annually.

#### All developing countries have expressed the need for additional international financial support to reach their country specific (CS) and nationally determined contributions (NDCs) under the Paris Agreement - as there are limited public resources to do so. As such, many have made additional funding requests in their NDCs. The GGC is mobilising climate financing from developed countries to support these ambitions they set forth in their NDCs. This is typically local of private sector and sub-sequence climate change efforts.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V8.0 | PAGE 9

## Proposal Introduction

### 1.38 In December 2020, the Global Financial Markets Association (GFMA), which represents the common interests of the world's leading financial and capital market participants to provide a collective voice on matters that support global capital markets, published its "Roadmap for Climate Finance." The GFMA roadmap outlines a number of recommendations including: (i) establishing a dedicated guarantee facility to mobilize private sector investment in climate projects; (ii) facilitating debt instruments that deploy risk mitigation, concessionary equity or mezzanine capital, PPIAs, and other mechanisms); (iii) the GCF is a solution to this market failure and will deploy the under-used financial instrument of credit guarantees. The GCF will be the first-ever guarantee company in the hard currency global capital markets focused on developing countries.

### 1.39 As can be seen in Figure 8, the existing dedicated guarantee providers are focused on local currency and are not yet up to scale for climate projects and programmes in beneficiary markets. The GCF will be the first ever guarantee company to exist in the hard currency global capital markets focused on developing countries.

### 1.40 As can be seen in Figure 8, the existing dedicated guarantee providers are focused on local currency and are not yet up to scale for climate projects and programmes in beneficiary markets. The GCF will be the first ever guarantee company to exist in the hard currency global capital markets focused on developing countries.

#### Figure 8: Market mapping existing provider services

| Country | Africa Guarantee Fund | Credit Insurance Investment Facility | GuaranteCo |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Local Capital Markets | Not Applicable | Product Gap | Global Capital Markets |

### 1.41 Envisioned as a self-sustaining programme with no defined "Horizon" but an exit horizon for GCF within 20 years as discussed in Section B6, GCF will, at the outset, provide guarantees to investments in sectors congruent with the Green Climate Fund's (GCF) strategic objectives. These include: MRA1 – renewable energy generation and access; MRA2 – low-emission transport; MRA3 – buildings, cities, industries, and appliances; ARAP – health, water, sanitation, and hygiene; and MRAR – agriculture.

### 1.42 As such, the focus or targeting considerations for sectors, countries, types of projects (integration or adaptation) and project products (grants, loans, or guarantees) will be determined by the GCF's strategic objectives. The GCF will provide a portfolio of green bonds or loans that deliver long-term debt funding from developed countries to developing countries. These products will be structured in such a way as to ensure that they are aligned with the GCF's strategic objectives and deliver significant climate impact in the relevant sectors. Hence the TSP will be guided by a Transaction Scorecard (Annex 2A) which will be used to determine the transaction's alignment with the GCF's strategic objectives.

### 1.43 As the GCF is a global programme without specific sub-projects identified yet, detailed studies demonstrating the most appropriate use of guarantees for each sector and country are not yet available. However, in order to facilitate intervention, including baseline information and the corresponding methodologies used to derive such information, will be required. This will include the development of a Transaction Scorecard (Annex 2A) which will be aligned with GCF's objectives as described in its Governance Policy (Annex 2B) and with the criteria reflected in the Transaction Sustainability Framework.

### 1.44 As of October 11, 2022, it received 9 NGLs from Gabon, India, Indonesia, the Philippines, Brazil, Lao, Rwanda and Tanzania. The GCF is also in discussions with other countries including Bangladesh, China, Ethiopia, Ghana, Kenya, Nigeria, Pakistan, South Africa, and Uganda. These countries, amongst others, offered an aggregated significant interest of 10% of the total portfolio size of the GCF.

Note that the purpose of most calculations underlying the funding proposal, the breakdown for the GCF's operation was presented previously to be in US dollars. The calculation is based on a USD/GBP exchange rate of 1:0.85. The calculations are linked to the typology of the project and sector rather than a prescribed timeframe. The lifespan of the GCF is not critical to the estimations.

The ToC diagram and narrative may include a wide range of co-benefits (as applicable) in the form of co-financing, co-investment, or other forms of support from public and private sector actors. How GCF will issue their Guarantees

1.46 Guarantees are a credit enhancement product. More technically, a guarantee is a legally binding agreement under which the guarantor agrees to pay up to all or an amount due on a loan, equity, or other instrument in the event of non-performance by the borrower. The guarantor can be any institution (public or private) that has the ability to provide relevant financial, ecological, institutional, etc.) that need to be addressed as well as risks and assumptions.

1.47 As such, a typical guarantee transaction is formed via a tri-partite relationship as shown in the diagram below:

| Project Borrower | Guarantor | Investors |
| --- | --- | --- |

1.48 To demonstrate how this works in practice, assume there is a company who wants to raise financing for a climate project that will be implemented by them and they are concerned about the ability of the company to repay the debt due to its perception of the riskiness of the climate project or the lack of financial resources. The company can seek to secure a guarantee from an institution (public or private) with a long-term debt to the climate project. It is more certain that the country will be repaid than it would be if no guarantee was provided.

1.49 Typically, the agreements that are signed when issuing a guarantee include 1) a guarantor agreement; and 2) a recourse agreement. The guarantor agreement sets out the terms of the guarantee itself (e.g., amount guaranteed, term of the guarantee). A recourse agreement is signed between guarantor company and the borrower (being either the climate project or the company implementing it), which sets out how the funds will be repaid in case of default. In other words, if the borrower defaults on the debt in the event of non-payment by the borrower, the guarantor has the right to recover these amounts from the borrower.

1.50 Once the guarantee is issued (and the recourse agreement signed), the borrower will pay the guarantor an ongoing guarantee fee which is payable throughout the life of the guarantee, typically on a quarterly basis in advance.

1.51 In addition to the ongoing guarantee fee, the borrower must also provide periodic financial statements and up-to-date reports to the guarantor. This enables the guarantor to assess the likelihood of a call on the guarantee. The guarantor will then decide whether or not it is willing to make a payment in case of default. They will be responsible for managing the relationship with the beneficiaries of the guarantee and the borrowers.

What happens in the event of a call? (This section covers at a high level what would happen in the event of a call on a project borrower’s guarantee)

1.52 A guarantor would ensure part of its terms and conditions that it is given much notice as possible before a call date, which will allow them to make an informed decision about whether or not they are willing to make a payment (in the context of lenders) or bond buyers (in the event the guarantee guaranteed a bond). From an institutional investor’s

## 1.54
Through this process there is a strong investment case for private sector participation in adaptation projects and activities. The GCF can provide an important role in supporting such investments by providing risk mitigation, including the avoidance of climate risks and the opportunities to develop new products/services and to reach new markets. The use of guarantees can also be used to de-risk climate investments for the private sector, thereby encouraging the entrance of new market actors.

## The Theory of Change is then built off of this concept:

1. A theory of change that is based on a vision and encompasses mobilizing private debt capital towards climate change mitigation and adaptation projects that deliver climate change solutions and environmental benefits.
2. The Theory of Change is designed to enable the GCF to stage the various outcomes the GCF aims to achieve through ensuring guarantees.

## Three preconditions the equity will be enabled:
- Equity capital released from the GCF will be enabled through three mechanisms provided by the GCF:

1. 1) Credit insurance and associated risk management (such as active portfolio management, restructuring and recovery of paid claims).
2. 2) Financial structuring (a problem-solving tool to bridge requirements of investors and borrowers); and
3. 3) Disbursement of technical assistance funds from the GCF to support countries in developing their own grant funded technical assistance facility to support the delivery of these mechanisms on the ground in developing countries.

## Climate and mitigation potential

1.56 It is important to note that a guarantee does not in itself make an unviable project good one. As such GCF funds will be used to provide guarantees for projects that are viable but have been deemed as being non-viable or less viable by global credit and capital markets due to some level of information asymmetry. Using the three mechanisms described above, the GCF can help to mitigate this risk and reduce the cost of capital associated with the climate project with the expectation that investors become more educated about the risks. They will gain in confidence as they see the results of the projects and the positive impact on the environment.

1.57 As mentioned earlier, it is expected that the annual climate finance need in developing countries will be USD 20 billion by 2030 to support the private sector in global credit and capital markets today a paramount to meeting the climate finance needs of tomorrow.

1.58 Indeed, a guarantee also has the potential to deliver on both direct and indirect climate outcomes for the participating countries. By providing guarantees to projects that are deemed non-viable by global credit and capital markets but have equity to operationalize and issue guarantees, participating countries will have reduced barriers (including investor risk appetite) in accessing finance from global credit and capital markets. This is particularly relevant given the GCF’s target of 2031 to invest in climate mitigation and adaptation interventions. With decreased barriers to receive investment for the investments, it is expected that there would be an increase in the number of projects being implemented, which will lead to increases resilience and the adaptive capacity of communities in the project region and mitigate intervention interventions tailored to reduce vulnerability. The GCF’s approach also seeks to leverage private sector capital by providing guarantees to financial markets whereas GCFs own approach intends to utilize finance from global capital markets. There is a growing demand for climate finance that can be met through the GCF, which will scale the private sector investment needed to prepare and implement much needed climate related projects/programmes.

1.58 Through its guarantee, GCF indirectly aims to reduce climate vulnerabilities for beneficiaries by guaranteeing projects with high mitigation potential. The government of Indonesia is working to improve access to finance for clean energy and sustainable transport sector projects geared toward improving the quality of life in Indonesia by investing in innovative non-sovereign green bond, the ProGreen Bond, which will be listed on the climate change market and encourage financial institutions to redirect their investments from fossil fuel financing into green financing. ### 1.59 GGC will work with Origination Partners (see Annex 23 for key characteristics/capabilities) to identify and build a pipeline of transactions that align with the eligibility criteria in the GGC Guarantee Policy (Annex 23) and using the TSP Annex (Annex 24A) and Transaction Scorecard (Annex 24B).

This will help Borrowers close together and use the global network to share best practices in terms of overcoming the challenges that are experienced when structuring transactions for climate finance solutions across developing countries. Capacity building is a key element of GGC’s mandate as it aims to build the capacity of developing countries to access climate finance on an equitable share of the climate finance available in global credit and capital markets. The fact that GGC can also use its money market instruments to raise short-term investments through its guarantees will boost credibility with global credit and capital markets.

The transaction scorecard will emphasize key components of GGC’s Guarantees Policy and Transaction Scorecard.

As recommended, knowledge generation and education of investors in global credit and capital markets is the overarching sustainable impact that GCC expects to deliver through its activities. GCC expects that all of the bonds issued under the Scheme are certified by the Climate Bonds Standard and Certification Scheme (‘Scheme’). The Scheme has a two-stage certification process with a pre- and post-issueance step. This involves an initial assessment to determine whether the project meets the criteria for inclusion in the Scheme. A comparative analysis of the Scheme versus other standards available in the global green finance market is provided.

Recognizing that borrowers may be willing to meet the reporting requirements of GCC but simply lack the capacity to do so, GCC will seek to work with borrowers using in-country consultants where necessary, to help them implement the required reporting and monitoring systems. The data generated by these monitoring and reporting tools will also be aggregated on the digital platform thereby allowing GCC to track its portfolio against agreed targets for greenhouse gas emissions reductions and other relevant climate parameters. This information then feeds into the annual review of GCC’s Transaction Scorecard (Annex 24B) and Guaranteed Policy (Annex C28), enabling a process of continuous incremental improvement.

## 1.65 Resource Mobilization: The International Finance Corporation and Amundi Asset Management identify the following as being their key barriers to greater investment being mobilised from the global green finance market into developing countries.

### Table 1: Key Barriers to green bond and loan issuance from developing countries

| Market Barrier | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Transparency | The limited capacity of borrowers in developing countries to communicate to provide reports to identify, measure, and disclose green investments which meet global market standards. |
| Supply constraints | Limited availability of dedicated green assets and a pipeline of green projects of scale. |
| Capacity | Lack of understanding how developing country borrowers and investors assess the quality and impact of green products. |
| Stability | Negative perception of investors in global credit and capital markets of the overall macroeconomic and policy stability of developing countries. |
| Liquidity | In combination the above four barriers create information asymmetry between borrowers and investors, which can lead to a lack of liquidity for green bonds and loans. |

Source: IFC, Bloomberg, Environmental Finance, Climate Bonds Initiative

## 1.66 The market barriers identified above continue to enshrine confidence and disincentivize investors in global credit and capital markets from investing in green bonds and loans. This is an essential ingredient for building and transforming markets and it is a diverse form of investment between the institutional capital or retail investor. In addition, there are also other factors that can influence the decision-making process of investors to issue credit arrangements which help investors in global credit and capital markets to overcome these barriers and bridge this gap. These include: (i) The use of green bonds as an alternative to traditional debt instruments; (ii) The issuance of green bonds by a borrower with a strong track record of sustainability performance, which can lead to the improvement of the credit rating of a capital market instrument (e.g., a bond). In simple words, if a bond was issued by a company that has a strong track record in terms of environmental and social responsibility, it may be rated from BBB- to BBB+ (the theoretical investment grade credit rating) on the basis. The link below provides an example of how Assured Sustainability Association (ASA) can help investors in global credit markets to overcome this barrier by issuing a project in Spain taking its project credit rating from BBB- to AAA by providing a guarantee for its bond.

### Table 2: How GGC will address market barriers between global credit and capital markets and developing countries

| Market Barrier | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Transparency | GGC will become a risk-sharing partner for investors by providing them with a total investment proposition that is better suited to the investor’s appetite, and drive quality reporting on the financial performance and impact of the borrower or the underlying projects to help address information asymmetry. Additionally, a key element of GGC’s value proposition will be the use of a digital platform that can help borrowers in developing countries to communicate with investors in global credit markets about their needs and the underlying projects supported. This database of climate projects in developing countries will help overcome the lack of liquidity for green bonds and loans by providing information on the quality, scale, and impact of these projects. |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V9.0 | PAGE 19

## Market Barrier: Supply Constraints

### Description
Limited availability of labelled green assets and a pipeline of green projects of scale.

### GGC Solution
Just by existing, GGC creates an incentive for strong borrowers to consider developing green projects in their countries. The Green Climate Fund (GGC) has already signed up 100+ countries as potential borrowers which highlights USD 2 billion of immediate opportunity for GGC. Once GGC is on the ground and lending to these countries, it will be able to generate confidence amongst developers of green projects to build more projects. The sponsors of GGC’s portfolio will also have access to a new source of funding that can be used in combination with CCGF and CGGF.

### Market Barrier: Capacity

#### Description
Lack of awareness and know how of developing country borrowers and investors about issuing and investing in green products.

#### GGC Solution
GGC will work closely with governments in developing countries, to help build the necessary assessment and reporting frameworks and their capacity to engage constructively with investors in global capital markets. Additionally, GGC will use its guarantee to help investors in the City of London to better understand the risks associated with investing in green bonds from developing countries. This will reduce their risk appetite for issuing bonds in developing countries now. The presence of liquidity in the City of London will also provide a more stable and liquid market for these bonds, which results in progression of capacity across a wider pool of investors in global capital markets.

## Market Barrier: Stability

### Description
Negative perception of Green Bond markets in developed markets on the overall macroeconomic and policy instability of developing countries.

#### GGC Solution
GGC will use its guarantee to help issuers from one of the international credit rating agencies and use its guarantee to provide investors with greater confidence that they will be protected from the perceived risks associated with investing in green bonds from developing countries.

## Market Barrier: Liquidity

### Description
In combination the above four barriers creates information asymmetry between borrowers and investors which reduce market appetite and thus liquidity for Green Bonds issued from developing countries. This is because of the lack of a secondary market, the lack of credit enhancement and use of a loan portfolio to help create more investment opportunities of scale for investors in the global capital markets.

#### GGC Solution
GGC will use its guarantee to provide investors with greater confidence that they will be protected from the perceived risks associated with investing in green bonds from developing countries. This will reduce their risk appetite for issuing bonds in developing countries now and a liquid portfolio to help create more investment opportunities of scale for investors in the global capital markets.

The additionality of guarantees lies in their unparalleled ability to build the capacity of global investors to overcome information asymmetry and for them to invest in future green bonds from developing countries without the need for credit enhancement.

## Table 4: GCF will be established based on the following design principles

| Design Principle | Description |
| --- | --- |
| **Globally supporting a programme with a global impact** | GCF is designed to support various types of projects for both mitigation and adaptation purposes on a global basis and once established can provide funding assistance in all countries. |
| **Multi-jurisdictional and multi-sectorial coverage of countries** | GCF will support the highest of the respective country’s climate priorities (with NGA inputs) with the greatest impact input by forming large portfolios of projects seeking funding/financial assistance. |
| **Improved financing modalities** | Delivery of lower “at” interest cost or fixed rates for longer term lending to 10-20 years uncommitted by GCF and/or other financial institutions. |
| **Sinking fund to make projects bankable and provide liquidity for bankable projects** | Combining project and credit structuring capabilities to make projects viable for guarantee GCF to be seen as a credible partner, capable of delivering high additionality to the climate financing space, in both developing countries and global markets. |
| **Support capacity to implement projects on demand request** | GCF aims to drive the standards and quality of assessment and reporting of NGA’s on a digital platform, with the aim of providing a single point of entry for all project requests. |
| **Incentive support for smaller countries (including Small Island Nations) to access global credit and capital markets by applying aggregation structures and vehicles that can help create the scale required** | GCF will look to support smaller countries (including Small Island Nations) to access global credit and capital markets by applying aggregation structures and vehicles that can help create the scale required. |

### 1.87 Accredited Entity (AE)
- MUFG Bank ("MUFG") - MUFG Bank, formerly the Bank of Tokyo-Mitsubishi UFJ, Ltd., is an international private sector entity with a long history in Japan. As one of the largest commercial banks in Japan, MUFG Bank provides comprehensive financial services and products globally through a variety of subsidiaries and branches. The bank has extensive experience and expertise in financing projects related to renewable energy including hydropower, geothermal, solar power and wind, and is also experienced in financing projects for sustainable agriculture and other non-financial businesses, as well as in supporting a sustainable society. MUFG has two GCF Funded projects: FP1713 and FP278.
- MUFG, as AE will oversee and monitor the project implementation and will ensure project compliance with MUFG's own internal policies and procedures. The AE will also be responsible for the obligations which it has under its Accredited Master Agreement (Implementation Agreement) with EE (DGG, GGC, GCF Holdings and GCF Holdings Manager) and this agreement is subject to the terms of the Funded Activity Agreement between the Funded Activity Agent (the Board of Director of GGC), MUFG as AE can directly receive information only relevant to shareholders and review the information provided by the Funded Activity Agent. The AE will also be responsible for the obligations which it has under such an agreement by capturing more substantive information related to GGG's operation.
- First, MUFG’s Emerging Market team based in London was selected to act as a bond originator. In 2015, after the project was awarded, the team was awarded, the team will act as a bond originator. ## 1.8 Existing Entities (EE)

DGG was founded by Boek Hooi Kho, Dale Pereira and Laetitia Perez and Cardano Development BV ("CD"), a Netherlands based company, which is the majority shareholder of DGG. CD has a global network of experts in setting up and running financial companies and companies focused on addressing development challenges.

## 1.8 Existing Entities (EE)

CD is the manager for several existing specialist guarantees focused on emerging markets such as GuarCo and Frontier; as well as developing new specialist guarantors, such as AgriCo and OctoCo. CD will be involved in the management of GGC and its subsidiaries. The key strength of DGG and CD lies in their extensive experience in the activities of GCG and to participate in its operations. A key strength of DGG is risk management expertise, both from a financial and operational perspective. DGG has over 10 years' experience in managing guarantees on behalf of clients globally, and has over USD 3 billion of capital under management. CD will be an active rather than passive shareholder in DGG and its subsidiaries. The combination of the two entities is expected to create significant synergies for GGC. DGG frequently creates independent management companies to house the teams of different initiatives that it is supporting and as such DGG has a number of other specialist guarantees.

Boek Hooi was CEO Vice President, Operations at the Credit Guarantees & Investment Facility (CGIF) - the guarantor set up under the auspices of the Asian Development Bank and was previously Deputy CEO of Cagayan, an infrastructure development company in the Philippines.

Dale and Laetitia were previously employees of CD. Dale acted as CFO and CEO of GuarCo and Frontier respectively; a specialist local currency focused guarantee focused on developing local capital markets in Africa and Asia that is part of the CGIF group. Laetitia was previously Head of Operations at CGIF. Both have extensive experience in the development of guarantees, particularly in relation to strategy, which resulted in the setting up of specialist insurance guarantees, InfraCo Nigeria and infraZim Pakistan.

GGC will also be an EE, together with GFG Holdings and GFG Holdings Manager:

## 1.9 Activity 1.1 - Formation and capitalisation of Green Guarantee Company Limited - (Implemented by DGG, GGCoJ

Following the completion on a procurement process conducted by the EEC the international law firm, Gowling WLG, has been selected to provide legal services in relation to the formation of GGC and its subsidiaries.

GGC has been incorporated in United Kingdom as a limited liability company and will have two core activities:

1. To issue shares to its equity shareholders and on that basis be equity-backed.
2. Issue guarantees on behalf of Borrowers in developing countries seeking to raise green bonds or loans to finance climate mitigation and adaptation projects.

GGC itself will not have any full-time employees or staff but will have a Board of Directors (the "Board") appointed by GGC's shareholders, which will be responsible for the management of GGC. The Board will be responsible for approving, appointing and delegating decision-making authority to DGG as Manager ("Manager") to manage the day-to-day operations of GGC. The Board will also be responsible for setting the strategic direction and approve the annual strategy and budget for GGC proposed by the Manager and to monitor and evaluate the performance of the Manager in relation to that strategy and budget.

The Board will consist of three members, two of whom are equity shareholders and providing them with periodic progress updates and ensuring their priorities are appropriately considered in the context of the overall strategy. The third member is an independent director who has a wealth of experience in this field, with at least three members expected to have relevant climate impact experience in addition to ESG and general governance expertise.

The three GGC Board members with climate, EGS and gender experience will be independent members of the GGC Impact and Credit Committees providing review and challenge to the DGG Impact Team in their assessment of potential climate projects and having a veto over which projects are progressed to a final approval. The anticipated profiles of the Board:

## B

### GCF Holdings will be incorporated as a special purpose vehicle. GCF Holdings will be solely owned by GCF and will receive all funding agreements from GCF. The GCF Board will approve the initial capital structure for daily operations of GCF Holdings including establishing a bank account, preparing financial statements, and receiving and sending payments.

Both GCF Holding and GCF Holdings Management will be EE.

### DGG will negotiate service contracts with the following key service providers for GGC:

* An international credit rating agency - GGC requires an investment grade credit rating from one of the three international credit rating agencies (Fitch’s Moody’s and S&P Global) to support its guarantee activities. GGC will pay an annual fee for this service.
* A third-party investment manager - GGC will engage a third party investment manager with strong track record (the example PMCO). The investment managers will be given an investment mandate by the Board of Directors, which is based on the approved Investment Policy Statement and the approved Investment Strategy. This document demonstrates that the portfolio of investments has been invested in line with the investment mandate and the Manager and Company’s risk appetite. An indicative investment mandate has been included in GGC’s Treasury Policy which sets out a strategic asset allocation for GGC’s equity and fixed income portfolios, as well as an indication of liquidity. Prior to any upon call guarantee, the Board of GGC shall instruct the investment manager to invest at least 10% of the guaranteed capital into liquid assets (e.g., cash or government bonds) with high liquidity.
* A third-party custodian - The custodian will be responsible for managing the underlying investments and ensuring that all transactions are executed in accordance with the agreed terms. GGC will pay an annual fee to the custodian.

### 2. Third party investment managers – as guarantees are unfunded until called, GGC’s equity capital and related assets will be invested by third-party investment managers (e.g., PMCO). The investment managers will be selected based on their track record of strong returns. The investment managers will be given an investment mandate by the Board of Directors which is based on the approved Investment Policy Statement and the approved Investment Strategy.

### GGC is targeting to leverage its equity capital up to 10x, meaning for every USD 1 of capital with GGC it will use USD 10 of guarantees. Given this unique ability to leverage, GGC does not require all of the capital invested in its 15-year business plan to be funded by GCF. The target leverage ratio is expected to be achieved through a phased approach as follows:

* Stage 1: Raise USD 10 million out of equity capital to enable commencement of operations.
* Stage 2: Raise USD 10 billion by Year 3 of the business plan to support growth to USD 2 billion portfolio.
* Stage 3: Raise USD 5 billion by Year 4 of the business plan to support growth to USD 7.5 billion portfolio.
* Stage 4: Raise USD 5 billion by Year 5 of the business plan to support growth to USD 10 billion portfolio.

### Stage 1

GGC has obtained approvals in principle from FCDO for USD 2 million and is actively working to raise a further USD 9 million to support its first tranche of equity, which will be a contribution of USD 40 million from GCF. GGC is in position to achieve first close by the end of 2021 and will commence writing guarantees in early 2023 with a guarantee capacity of USD 1 billion (USD 100 million - explained in Section A2).

### Stage 2

GGC is expected to meet most of its initial guarantee capacity of USD 1 billion within two years of commencing operations and consequently the Manager will continue to function in parallel to securing guarantees with a further USD 975 million. GGC expects to have a total equity capital of USD 400 million by Year 2, which will provide an additional USD 3.7 billion guarantee capacity (USD 1 billion + USD 2.7 billion - explained in Section A2).

## 1.0 Activity 2 - Secure investment grade credit rating for GGC (Implemented by DGG and GSG)

To obtain a credit rating on its GGC will be necessary for the Manager to engage one or more international credit rating agencies to rate the GGC. The rating process is likely to involve an assessment of the GGC’s financial strength, based upon GGC’s business plan and how those assumptions align with the rating methodology that has been chosen. The outcome of this process may result in a positive rating (indicating that the GGC meets the criteria for investment grade) or a negative rating (indicating that the GGC does not meet the criteria). In either case, the process of converting an indicative rating to a public rating is quite essential as most of the analysis has been carried out by the rating agency. The credit rating will be necessary for GGC to enter into a rating agreement with the international rating agency and pay an annual rating fee.

## 1.92 Activity 1.4 - Ensure report on effectiveness of guarantees (Implemented by DGG and GSG)

As mentioned above, liquidity management and maintaining confidence are both critical to the success of a guarantee such as this one. The GGC will be able to manage its liquidity risk by ensuring that it has sufficient funds available in advance of them maturing and to be prepared to make payments when they become due in a timely manner; thereby establishing a relationship with the guarantor and securing its reputation.

Effective project management involves several lines of communication between the Borrower through regular monitoring of its financial impact and KPIs. This close working relationship is essential for a successful guarantee transaction.

## B.5 Justification for GCF funding request (max 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

*Explain why the project/programme requires GCF funding i.e., why is the project/programme not currently being financed by public or private sources?*

### 1.95 In the aftermath of the Global Financial Crisis, privately capitalized guarantees in the form of monoline guarantors all but disappeared from the market. The only major remaining provider was the International Monetary Fund (IMF) through its Credit Guarantee Facility which exposes to the toxic asset class was kept to the minimum. Since then, the guarantee modality has been taken up by a number of other institutions including the World Bank Group (WBG), European Investment Bank (EIB), African Development Bank (AfDB), (Guarantees and) IntraCredit Nigeria and local currency bond markets (Banpanim and Credit Guarantee and Investment Facility). The fact that private sector guarantees have been allowed to exist in this market has been a major factor in the low interest rates allowed by these lending agencies to maintain their high ratings have not allowed the demonstration of commercial viability for the GCF.*

### 1.96 GCF and the other Government's equity contribution to start-up GCF also provides confidence to rating agencies that rate GCF as it is backed by a sovereign country. However, in order to ensure that GCF can continue to be rated AAA and succeed, it is critical for the rating agencies to be convinced of GCF commitment towards its objectives and the policy and political will of the Government to support GCF. This is also reflected through the level of equity contribution to GCF. There are no expected threats from any other sources.*

# Reviving Guarantors

## As proven, specialist guarantors at financing mobilization mechanics can be a tool to fulfill with due care even for countries with limited experience in developing countries as export credit agencies accomplish this role.

### For more information, contact: Currently, government vehicles are not able to meet their objectives to use their existing resources for local borrowing. This is because increasing demand for their guarantees is outpacing the ability of governments to provide them. The GCF has sufficient allocation to fully support the establishment of a new hard currency guarantor for green finance. As such, GCF can be an important enabler in this regard and will continue to work with countries towards getting a solution.

### Remaining amounts are expected to be contributed by Governments including the UK and other donors.

## It is anticipated that GCG's public capital base equity needed to start-up its operations will be progressively combined with private sector contributions. The amount of private sector participation will depend on the level of commercial returns are given. When this occurs, GCF and GCG's Government shareholders would not only have sufficient resources to support the establishment of a new hard currency guarantor for green finance but also revive the guarantee modality sufficiently to attract private capital. New guarantees can then follow to meet the demand for financing.

## 1.00 With the goal of reviving the guarantee modality and attracting private capital, it is important for GCG to deliver financing solutions that market based with a minimum concessionality in each of the transactions as well as operating on a parallel basis with other financial instruments. The proposed financial instrument will be structured so that it can provide a partial credit support to the bonds issued by GCG. This will allow GCG to access the capital markets and up lift the credit profile of the bonds via the securities of guarantors, which requires equity contributions from GCF.

## B. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Explain how the programme/project will successfully exit once implementation is completed, including how results and benefits will be sustained over time.

### Sustainability of Impact

1.06 Unless a fund, GCF is not being set up with a defined lifespan but as a self-sustaining corporate that will seek to grow its portfolio of investments in order to generate returns for the investors. The amount of capital required to achieve this goal is estimated to be required by developing countries to invest in climate adaptation and mitigation projects up to 2030 it can be envisaged that GCF will have to continue to operate beyond 2030.

1.07 Consequently, GCF's impact potential could last for a significantly longer period than the envisaged investment from CCF and other funds. The exit strategy is therefore designed to ensure that GCF continues to make investments in projects with an existing and credible track record. By a single data point on report to ESD and climate type of the projects that GCF is guaranteeing it is anticipated that this will be sufficient for investors to understand the risks associated with the project, the development potential and impact with available cost-benefit analysis. This could have a triple effect: open up the governments of the developing countries, which are not yet ready to access capital markets, to attract private sector investment (up to 200 million) incentivizing them to adopt international standards at a national level to facilitate greater flows of capital.

1.08 Once this data is added to the platform it becomes part of a growing body of evidence as to the true underlying risks associated with climate change and its impact on developing countries. This will also provide a growing body of evidence that can help future climate projects in developing countries to attract capital from global credit and capital markets to fund their projects.

2.03 Resulting from the combination of (1) and (2) above GCF expects that over time market transformation will take place with investors in global credit and capital markets increasing their own capacity to understand and evaluate the risks associated with climate change and its impact on developing countries. In such a scenario an exit will come through GCF dropping leaving new guarantees and managing down its portfolio. As noted earlier, the development of the private sector in the field of infrastructure in developing countries is seen as a key element for infrastructure in the United States was not viewed as an attractive investment by investors who did not understand how to evaluate the risks associated with climate change and its impact on developing countries.

Exit Strategy

1.108 The business plan anticipates that GGC will be able to deliver a return on equity that will be attractive to commercial investors by Year 10 of operations. Consequently, GGC will seek to prepare an exit for the founding shareholders, including GFZ.

1.109 Should a public listing of GGC's equity in a global capital market (e.g., LSE) by Year 5 of operations. It is not anticipated that GGC will be able to achieve this goal within five years of operations. Instead, GGC will create a "shop window" for potential private sector investors to observe how GGC performs over a period of time.

2. Between Year 5 and Year 10, private sector investors will be sought to acquire the listed shares from the founding shareholders at a premium price.

1.110 Should a public listing of GGC's equity be deemed not viable at a future date then an alternative exit strategy will be for GGC to seek a strategic buyer that is willing to pay a premium price for its equity. The proceeds from such a sale would be bought by GGC at a premium from the shareholders, thereby increasing the economic value of the remaining shares, with no dilution in the shareholding of GFZ and other founding shareholders. It is anticipated that the proceeds from this sale will be USD 20 billion by Year 10 of operations. Conservatively assuming that GGC does not give its profits beyond the USD 5 billion to the founding shareholders, then the remaining equity would be worth USD 15 billion. This could be paid out as cash to shareholders over the following 10 years of operations. Consequently, GCF should at least have its initial capital investment returned by Year 20.

1.110 Should GGC prove unsuccessful in achieving its business plan then an option available to its Board and shareholders will be to sell all or part of the equity back to GFZ and other founding shareholders on a pro rata basis, thereby ensuring that time and GGC's capital is delivered it can be returned to the shareholders on a pro rata basis, thereby ensuring an orderly exit.

#### D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

##### 1.1.6 The GCC will contribute to five of the GCC’s results areas: MIRAI, MRRAJ, MRRAJ and ARAJ and ARAR.

###### 1.1.7 In terms of contribution to the GCF’s low-emission sustainable development pathways, the GCC will have an estimated mitigation impact of 20 million tonnes CO2e per year (based on a 5-year rolling average). The GCC will also contribute across all beneficiary countries (CORE 1; kindly refer to calculation methodology in Annex 22). It is anticipated that many mitigation projects will be implemented under the GCC, which will have an estimated cumulative impact of 30 million tonnes CO2e per year.

###### 1.1.8 In terms of contribution increased climate-resilient sustainable development, the GCC will have an estimated adaptation impact of 5 million people per year (based on a 5-year rolling average). The GCC will also contribute across all participating countries (CORE 2; kindly refer to calculation methodology in Annex 22).

###### 1.1.9 The GCC will also have a positive impact on the enabling environment for climate finance in participating countries, and among other things, it will help to build capacity of national governments and financial institutions for climate change transformation (CORE 7), making guarantees more visible and frequently used financial instruments to de-risk climate investments. It is anticipated that many adaptation projects will be implemented under the GCC, which will have an estimated cumulative impact of 10 million people per year.

###### 1.1.10 GCC’s ability to leverage is equity 10x+ (i.e., every USD 1 of additional equity contribution achieving USD 10 of guaranteed climate finance). The GCC will be able to achieve this level of leverage because it has a strong track record and would typically be possible via direct investment models. Furthermore, GCC’s function as a corporate entity allows it to be able to access a wide range of capital markets (e.g., debt, equity, private placement) and financial instruments that are available in the broad range of public and private sector investors. Once established as a demonstrated guarantee company able to accomplish its mission, the GCC will be able to attract a wide range of institutional investors, including those focused on climate changes (for example Climate Investor One. A climate focused fund supported by GCF has now created Climate Investment Funds (CIFs) in several countries).

###### 1.1.11 While GCC is not expected to participate in transactions directly involving other GCF initiatives, to avoid double-counting of resources and to ensure that the GCC’s unique role as a global climate change guarantee facility is preserved, it will be possible for the GCC to mobilize private sector climate finance at scale could enable the pathway to success established by other GCF-backed guarantees. The GCC will not replace or duplicate any existing GCF initiatives. Instead, the GCC will complement them. #### D.3 Sustainable development (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

To explore opportunities to share their knowledge and learning at a project level more widely with global credit and capital markets thereby enabling replication and maximising the initiators' collective impact.

The Fund will seek to engage with a range of potential borrowers in developing countries. The largest targets are likely to be limited local climate finance market depths and underdeveloped financial market infrastructure and frameworks to identify, certify and mobilise climate change projects. These include small and lower-middle-income countries (LICs) and Small Island Developing States (SIDS). In addition, the Fund will seek to engage with a range of potential borrowers in global credit and capital markets (so investment grade at a local level) to access global credit and capital markets where they would be competitive. The Fund will also seek to engage with a range of potential borrowers in global credit and capital markets and meeting the international standards required by global credit and capital markets in terms of quality and availability of information, including financial statements, governance structures, risk management frameworks, etc.

With the support of GCF, these borrowers, given their high profile in local credit and capital markets, can carry an important advocacy role for the Fund. The Fund will seek to engage with a range of potential borrowers in global credit and capital markets where they would be competitive. These borrowers are likely to have a strong track record of climate change projects and/or experience in mobilising private sector finance for climate change projects.

The Fund will also seek to engage with a range of potential borrowers in global credit and capital markets that provide a powerful need and resulting stimulus to national governments to create similar enabling environments needed to scale up climate change projects. These include countries where the government has made a strong commitment to scaling up climate change projects, but where there is a lack of enabling environment.

## Capital markets represent an important mobilization and deployment mechanism in the financing of the SDGs. This proposal outlines how the Green Climate Fund (GCF) can play a major opportunity to deploy capital at scale by harnessing the cumulative firepower of institutional investors.

### The public sector and private finance will be core to the implementation of the SDGs. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V9.0 | PAGE 40

## Benchmarking the target countries relative to all countries in terms of their climate change vulnerability, adaptive capacity, and readiness to access international climate finance.

The INFORM index indicates that most of the countries selected fall within the world's top third or top half of most vulnerable countries. The GCI index shows that most of these countries are also among those with the lowest levels of economic development. This means that many of them have pressing needs for investments that will reduce climate change vulnerability and/or enhance adaptive capacity and resilience.

### The flexibility of the guarantee product means that GCCF represents a unique opportunity for GCC to catalyze a programmatic approach to supporting developing countries in their efforts to access international climate finance, while also being cross-cutting and global in its mandate. For example, GCC has been asked to consider supporting the development of electric vehicle infrastructure in developing countries as part of the GCF's work on transport under the Sustainable Cities and Roads. Equally, opportunities have been identified for GCC to support the propagation of electric vehicles in India as part of the GCF's work on energy access.

### At COP26, developing countries were vocal about their need for greater volumes of climate finance to be mobilized from developed countries. The GCF has already committed to doubling its funding levels by 2025 (see the GCF’s 2021-2023 report titled “Climate Change 2022: Impacts, Adaptation and Vulnerability,” the Least Developed Countries Group on Climate Finance, and the Global Environment Facility). The GCF has also committed to mobilizing USD 4 billion by 2025. It was stated that it failed last year that developed countries failed in their commitment to deliver USD 100 billion in climate finance over a period of time. This is why GCCF is so important, as it will help to ensure that the GCF can continue to mobilize climate finance for developing countries.

### Unfortunately, global and geopolitical events such as the COVID-19 pandemic and conflict in Eastern Europe have put significant fiscal strain on both developed and developing countries alike, resulting in the former having to reduce the amount of climate finance they are able to provide. At the same time, the latter has had to divert resources from climate investment opportunities remains strong as demonstrated by the fact that despite the pandemic the global green bond market grew 20% year-on-year in 2021. This is because the GCF’s unique ability to leverage its equity and debt instruments to mobilize global capital markets, coupled with its track record of successfully mobilizing global multilateral investment from global credit and capital markets, makes it a uniquely positioned institution to help developing countries access climate finance.

### The GCCF will be structured as an equity fund managed by Lazard. It is expected that the GCCF will be funded over time through contributions from developed countries, with the initial funding coming from the GCF. The GCCF will also have the ability to raise additional capital in global credit and capital markets. This will allow it to scale up its operations more quickly than a typical development fund.

### As the first and only guarantee dedicated to supporting developing countries to build bridges with global capital and credit markets, GCCF has several unique advantages over other climate finance mechanisms:

1. **Lack of perceived risk in projects in developing countries:** GCC will help identify and structure climate change adaptation and mitigation projects that are attractive to investors.
2. **Perceived ratio of missing in developing countries generally:** By providing its guarantee GCC is risk-sharing with the private sector, which can help to overcome concerns about investing in a developing country.
3. **Local capacity of credit and capital markets in developing countries:** The GCCF will work closely with local financial institutions to educate them on terms of understanding and meeting the international standards required by global credit and capital markets. This will allow GCCF to scale up its operations more quickly than other climate finance mechanisms.

### As mentioned earlier, the success of local borrowers raising long-term climate finance from global credit and capital markets is a key driver for scaling up GCCF’s operations. The GCCF will also help to create similar enabling environments needed to scale local climate finance markets which both local and, in time, global investors can access.

**D**

*Please define how the beneficiary country (less ownership of and implements the funded project/programme). Discuss the following:*
- Existing climate strategy: Existing GCF programme.
- Relevance to and alignment with existing policies such as National Determined Contributions (NDCs), National Adaptation Plan (NAPs), and National Action Plans (NAPs).
- Alignment with other relevant international commitments, including those under the Paris Agreement.
- Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders.

### 1.137
Engagement with the NDA and relevant stakeholders in a target country will be a continuous and ongoing process for GCF given that it is not limited to a single transaction in each target country but is able to consider multiple projects over several decades. The GCF will engage with the NDA and relevant stakeholders as well as considering each country's NDCs and Country Programmes.

### 1.138
To this end, the GCF has engaged with countries such as Bangladesh, Bhutan, Cambodia, India, Indonesia, Kenya, Lesotho, The Philippines, Rwanda, South Africa, Timor-Leste & Uganda. These consultations were conducted in accordance with the GCF’s Country Programme Development (CPD) and National Strategy and Policy (NAPs) 25 which, as mentioned earlier, seeks alignment with a target country's NDC and Country Programme. While these engagements are not exhaustive, they have been useful to identify opportunities for engagement that will be focused on financing small climate projects that are more prevalent in LDCS and SIDS as a key element of its growth strategy.

### 1.139
From a transaction perspective, GCF will be conducting country dialogues on every opportunity it generates. The GCF will also engage with the NDA and relevant stakeholders to ensure alignment with the GCF’s Own Environmental and Social Standards (ESS) and will engage with local stakeholders, including vulnerable communities in LDCS and SIDS, to ensure that the project is aligned with their needs and priorities. The Country Programme Assessment and Action Plan is in place and it aligns with the requirements set out by both the GCF (as a UNFCCC entity) and the NAPs. The GCF will also engage with local stakeholders on gender equality and women’s empowerment. To ensure that the local perspectives on a potential climate project is understood, the GCF will engage with local communities in LDCS and SIDS to ensure alignment with their needs and priorities.

### D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max: 500 words, approximately 1 page)
Deliverables and outputs of the proposed project/programme, including addressing existing bottlenecks/another barrier, and providing the minimum concessionality to ensure the project is viable without excluding funding from other sources.

#### 1.140
The targeted equity cap for GCF of USD 350 million is sized as the optimal amount on the assumption that with 10 times leverage, a USD 2.5 billion pool of guarantees can be supported at GCF origin. On the basis that single guarantee providers will not be able to provide such a large pool of guarantees, the equity cap is set at USD 350 million. The transaction ticket size will allow for GCF to support sizable bond and loan transactions as well as small transactions that would not be feasible for a single provider due to high underwriting fees and/or low credit risk.

#### 1.141
As mentioned previously, expansion of equity in GCF is required to provide the necessary pool of capital as well as other contributors. GCF will not be crowding out any private sector or public sector guarantors or financiers especially when the anticipated projects will have access to long-term funding from the capital markets without GCF’s assistance.

### Please describe how the proposed project/programme is efficient, and provide evidence that it complies with applicable benchmarks.
#### 1.142
Overall, USD 85 million GCF funding will deliver mitigation benefits at USD 95 per ton (GCF contribution / expected lifetime emission reductions).

#### 1.143
It is taking into account revenue recycling of equity contribution to support a revolving guarantee portfolio (including and excluding the USD 60 million in equity) that is expected to generate USD 2 billion in total over the next ten years, with an average annual growth rate of 5% per year and an average lifetime of 17.5 years. The GCF is also expected to issue USD 4.9 billion in guarantees in total over the next 10 years. From this 22.5% increase in total guarantees issued against the USD 4 billion guarantee pool size on a rolling basis, GCF has the potential to deliver mitigation.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V9.0 | PAGE 42

#### 1.144
**Leverage**
The Fund will use USD 8.3 million from GCF up to a rolling portfolio of USD 4 billion or 48-times leverage. Based on total guarantee issued and the assumption of GGC’s conservative portfolio growth in the initial years of USD 4.9 billion, this implies that the Fund will be able to leverage its resources by 50 times.

**Mobilization of private insurance and reinsurance markets**
Although not yet certain, plans to introduce private insurance cover for both political risks and quota share credit risk cover all imply a significant increase in the level of private sector participation. This is likely to come from direct participation from the commercial insurance and reinsurance markets. This cannot be accurately measured at this moment but will certainly encourage insurers to expose their balance sheets more fully.
