There is no ability to compare rates of return with and without GCFs support as there would be minimum concessionality as well as a significant increase in the level of private sector participation of capital for GCF to build up its operations and focus on supporting the most critical climate interventions needed.

## E

GCC and IFC/IDB exit for GCF P-12 (Yr 2020-2042)

GCF and IFC/IDB will either be listing the shares of GCC on a public market or via a sale to a private sector third party. The ability to access larger pools of equity capital from private investors is expected to increase GCC’s ability to attract additional funds into global credit and capital markets to increase flows of climate finance into developing countries.

### 2. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### Particulars of the monitoring, reporting and evaluation plan

GCC's monitoring and evaluation plan is intended to help investors and project teams (i.e., Borrowers) to establish effective monitoring and reporting protocols that provide consistent, comparable data on progress towards agreed outcomes, as well as information about the investments such as those described above. The approach will be designed to support gender and market transformation. GCC will also track their progress in meeting their programme specific indicators. The system will require real-time monitoring approaches to demonstrate progress against key milestones and targets. This will allow GCC to provide investors with a clear picture of how their investment is being managed on a real-time from their investments.

This approach is based on proactive monitoring of key issues, challenges and risks that may arise during the selection process.

#### Parties responsible:

GCC will be responsible for overseeing and reporting to the investor (for example to GCF) on the progress made in the monitoring and evaluation plan. This task will be supported by the AE who will submit the overarching aggregated data report to the relevant stakeholders. GCC is also responsible for managing the system design, execution and maintenance of the monitoring and evaluation plan.

The Lead Borrower will be responsible for providing the necessary information to the investor on a regular basis (e.g., quarterly) in line with the agreed reporting schedule or their review. To ensure that any social, economic climate agenda indicators or targets are reported adequately and fully, a Climate Advisory Team will be appointed to assist with ensuring that the Borrower is able to provide adequate data for these indicators.

The Lead Borrower will also assist with collecting baseline data at a country level to ensure that recipient countries are designated projects which are country relevant. The team will not be responsible for providing any of the monitoring and evaluation data but will be responsible for ensuring that the Borrower has provided all necessary information in line with the reporting schedule.

The Lead Borrower will also be responsible for submitting the quarterly and annual reporting cycle. Borrowers will be expected during any reporting period to account for expected results targets as indicated per their logframe/RME plan or other agreed indicators. The Lead Borrower will be required to provide a full data collection protocol for their projects at the time of signing the agreement, which will include all necessary information and data points that are required in order to meet the monitoring and evaluation requirements.

The Lead Borrower will also be responsible for ensuring that the Borrower has provided adequate data for any social, economic climate agenda indicators or targets. The Lead Borrower will also be encouraged to consider the data they need to adequately and robustly complete reports. Whenever possible, all indicators should be reported on a quarterly basis with annual reporting as well.

## E

compares actual results with the set of pre-determined targets. If a material factor is not covered or omitted, GCC will take the appropriate remediation steps which may include requiring Borrower to correct or undertake an independent assessment supported by the review of a neutral third party and/or local and/or external verifying firms.

GCC will evaluate and aggregate the data received from the Borrowers into a consolidated format. The consolidated report will be submitted to GCF for review and approval. GCC will provide an independent analysis of the outcomes received in the consolidated format.

GCC will review the consolidated report and if required, appoint a third party reviewer or auditor to review the outcomes (on an annual basis).

One year after the end of each financial year, the Borrower shall submit its annual report to GCC.

The final report will be shared on a shared database for use of reference by investors and GCFs internal team.

### Certification and annual reporting standards

Annual Reporting Standards are required under GCF’s Investment Requirements including:

- **E&S Risks Standard Reporting**: Includes: Business Description, Use of Proceeds, Applicable EAS Performance Standards, Corporate Governance practices, stakeholder consultations and stakeholder engagement process, and grievance redress mechanisms.
- **Climate Impact Reporting**: Includes: Climate Change Performance Indicators (CCPI) to be followed.

### Climate Impact reporting

- **Carbon Intensity**: Applies the EAS Performance Indicators (KPIs) for mitigation and adaptation for each investment including the nature of and number of beneficiaries and expected outcomes. The indicators will be based on the project’s carbon intensity, the portfolio and individual bond level.
- **SDGs and NDCs**: Adopt SDG-related as well as NDC-related performance indicators (both quantitative and qualitative), excepted results for each project sector and type of investment.

GCC aims to apply best practices in the developing countries in following best practices for the adoption of the Green Bond Principles, Green Loan Principles and/or other relevant principles. The GCC will ensure that the Borrower complies with the requirements set out by the Board of Directors of GCF and the terms of loans (the lenders).

The bonds will be independently certified (ongoing) by independent Approved Verifiers of the list of the Climate Bonds Standard and independently verified against the Climate Bonds Standard (version 3.0). The Climate Bond certification includes the analysis of compliance with the Green Bond Principles and the Green Bonds will be part of the Climate Bond Initiative’s Green Bond Index.

The Climate Bonds Taxonomy identifies the assets and projects needed to deliver a low carbon economy and gives GHG emissions screening criteria consistent with the 2 degree global warming target set by the COP17 Paris Agreement. The Taxonomy is the foundation used by the Climate Bonds Initiative to screen bonds to determine whether assets or projects are eligible for inclusion in its Green Bond Index. The Climate Bonds Initiative has developed the Taxonomy used by the Climate Bonds Initiative to screen bonds to determine whether assets or projects underlying an investment are eligible for green financing. While the Climate Bonds Initiative has developed Criteria for Certification, these sectors have not yet been certified under the Climate Bonds Standard and we accordingly do not provide our guarantee.

GCC’s Guarantee Policy is aligned with the Climate Bonds Taxonomy

## RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

### P.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

#### Selected Risk Factor: Pipeline

## Selected Risk Factor: Human resources

### Description
Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.
The scarcity of guarantees professionals locally means that there is a very shallow market for experienced investment professionals with guarantee experience. This means GGC will need to train up new guarantee professionals which could impact its ability to scale.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If "No", what then?
The co-founders of DGG have a proven track record in establishing and scaling guarantee companies including building teams of guarantees professionals.


## Selected Risk Factor: Lower Guarantee Capacity

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

### Description
Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.
GGC's inability to meet market demand will inhibit GGC from scaling up as quickly as forecasted. Failure to do this will impact profitability and the ability to absorb ECA losses.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If "No", what then?
GGC will need to scale up quickly as forecasted then DGG and GGC will review and revise origination strategy, initiate strategy to minimize any reputational risk and prioritize high return transactions to maximize revenue. DGG will also delay recruitment and underwrite cost base.

| Category | Probability | Impact |

Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If "No", what then?
If GGC's transactions do not meet key climate policy performance indicators (TPPI), GGC to review and revise origination strategy and review and revise GGC’s Guarantee Policy (Annex 23), Transaction Selection Process (Annex 24A) and Transaction Underwriting Process (Annex 25).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V9.0 | PAGE 66

Attach the appropriate assessment and management instruments or other studies, depending on the environmental and social risks of the project.

GGC has developed an E&S Framework based on international best practice which outlines the methodology of GGC to select bond investments that are aligned with its E&S Risk Standards and Requirements. The E&S Framework is designed to identify, assess, mitigate, and monitor any E&S risks and impacts. In addition to enabling GGC to demonstrate the impact outcomes it has on climate through its green finance, the E&S Framework also provides a structured approach for managing E&S risks in line with international best practice and GGC’s own internal policies and protocols. The framework comprises five core pillars, which together form the overarching framework, and equity contribute to GGC’s overall E&S objectives as outlined in Figure 2 below.

## Figure 2: GGC and E&S Framework

| Pillar | Description |
| --- | --- |
| **1\. Environmental & Social (E&S) Risk Management** | Ensures that all projects are assessed for their environmental and social risks and impacts, including the potential to cause harm. |
| **2\. Environmental & Social (E&S) Risk Standards and Requirements** | Focuses on how GGC’s TSP Annex 24A guided by its Transaction Scorecard (Annex 24B) will identify, assess, mitigate, and monitor E&S risks and impacts associated with the bonds and securities it invests in. |
| **3\. Environmental & Social (E&S) Risk Monitoring** | Includes a monitoring framework to ensure that GGC’s E&S risk management is effective and efficient. |
| **4\. Environmental & Social (E&S) Risk Reporting** | Provides guidance on how GGC will report its E&S risks and impacts in line with international best practice, including providing recommendations and guidance to borrowers in order to rectify any issues / gaps identified and improve E&S performance. |
| **5\. Environmental & Social (E&S) Risk Training** | Includes training for GGC staff on the E&S Framework, as well as training provided by third parties on labour conventions of the International Labour Organisation (ILO). Refer to Annex 3 of the GGC Funding Proposal for further details on the training programme. |

Key guidelines for applying the E&S framework are as follows:

- **GGC’s Guidance Policy Annex 23**: Transaction Selection Process (Annex 24A) and Transaction Scorecard (Annex 24B) will be used to identify, assess, mitigate, and monitor environmental and social risks. Projects with high E&S risk will require more detailed assessment.
- **GGC will conduct a rigorous assessment**, guided by its Transaction Selection Process (Annex 24A) and the Transaction Scorecard (Annex 24B), in order to ensure that projects are aligned with GGC’s E&S Risk Standards and requirements. The Transaction Scorecard serves as an initial screening tool for non-compliant transactions early on.
- **GGC will likely not have the opportunity** to influence the E&S assessment of a climate project at an early stage in the project development cycle; instead, GGC relies on having a robust TSP which will review and carry out stringent due diligence.

### G2. Gender assessment and action plan (inc. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Provide a summary of the gender assessment and project/programme-level gender action plan that is aligned with the objectives of the GCF’s E&S Management Framework. The gender assessment should be based on the latest available data and documents. Provide information on the key findings to inform and validate any and why key recommendations (how to address the underlying causes of gender inequality) are made, including how these recommendations will be integrated into the project design. Include inputs integrated into the action plan, including: how addressing the submatrixes will ensure equal participation and benefits from the project for women and men; how the GCF’s Gender Action Plan will be used to inform the project design; and any arrangements that the GCF has put in place to ensure activities are implemented and expected outcomes will be achieved. Consideration should also be given to the gender equality and women's empowerment indicators.

The GCC fully adheres to the principle of country ownership, meaning that for each project that receives a guarantee, the GCC will ensure that a Gender Assessment and Action Plan be put in place and be aligned with the requirements set out both by the GCF and the host country. The GCC will also ensure that the gender assessment is integrated into the project design to inform the gender equality and women's empowerment. More specifically, the GCC will ensure that:

- Project designs are based on a thorough analysis of the context, budgeting, staffing, implementation monitoring and evaluation. These assessments should be underpinned by collection and analysis of sex-disaggregated data and information on the social, economic and political environment in which the project is being implemented.
- Project action plans define the target population(s), and whether it is direct or indirect. Where possible, groups should be disaggregated further into identified subgroups such as women headed households.
- Project designs are informed by a gender analysis of the project’s context, budgeting, staffing, implementation monitoring and evaluation.
- Project owners have the requisite material, financial and human budgetary resources for the development and implementation of the action plan. The GCC will ensure that the project team is adequately trained on gender equality sensitive stakeholder engagements during project implementation and operation. Where technical assistance needs are identified, these should be included in the project’s budget.
- Project designs include a monitoring framework to track progress against the gender equality and women's empowerment indicators.

Given the GCC has not yet identified a pipeline of projects, it is not possible – to develop focused and detailed gender analyses, assessments, and action plans that the GCC would typically require for a Full Fund Proposal. However, the GCC will assess each project proposal in terms of its potential impact on gender equality and women's empowerment. The GCC will consider how the project design addresses the five key areas of results that GCF will look at to ensure that projects are aligned with the GCF’s Gender Action Plan. These include: (i) the extent to which the project promotes gender equality and women's empowerment issues in each of the GCF’s 5 countries of focus. These assessments inform the design of the project, including how the project will be implemented and monitored. The GCC will also ensure that a framework is put in place for monitoring progress against these indicators. This should be considered as part of the project design process to maintain the gender benefits of a specific project. The framework will include a set of gender equality and women's empowerment indicators, which are aligned with the GCF’s Gender Action Plan.

In the context of the above, the GCC recognizes the importance of ensuring sound gender practices in order to avoid and/or minimize potential financial and reputational liabilities and ensure benefits to women and girls are realized (and enhanced) through project design. The GCC will use the Gender Assessment and Action Plan as part of its due diligence process under the Due Diligence Process (Annex 24A) and related Transaction Scorecard (Anne 24B) which is used to select transactions that ensure key gender risks and impacts have been identified and are effectively managed throughout the transaction lifecycle. Some of the key factors:

There is no delegation of authority to DGGs which will be described in a Management Agreement and DGGs are staffed by a dedicated team of professionals with extensive experience in writing guarantees and development impact and will be managed by the GCF.

GCF will invest capital in highly rated, liquid high securities. This would be a regulated activity within the UK; however, the commercial intention is for GCF to outsource investment activities fully regulated third-party investment manager (e.g., PMCO, Fund of Funds, etc.) to manage the investments on behalf of GCF. The DGG will not have any direct regulatory or operational authorization. Depending on the type of instruments made by the third party investment manager, GGC may also need to appoint a credit committee and/or risk committee. In all cases, the DGG will be responsible for ensuring that the activities are carried out in line with GCF's policy procurement to GGC being capitalised and the successful funds will be approved by the Board of GGC. The DGG will have no direct regulatory or operational authorization over the third party investment manager. Under certain agreements, including a detailed investment policy, will be agreed prior to onboarding the third-party investment manager.

The DGG Executive team will meet regularly with the GGC Board to review the performance of GGC against key performance indicators (KPIs) and other metrics as appropriate. The KPIs will include but not limited to:

- Capital raised
- Number of transactions
- Portfolio composition
- Return on investment

The Chairperson of GGG and the Managing Partner of DGG will meet regularly with GGC's shareholders to discuss, among other things, the performance of GGC and members will have the ability to request information from DGG (with respect to GGC) as required.

GCG and DGG will have a Code of Conduct (setting out the minimum standards of ethical behavior required of both entities) which will be reviewed on an annual basis by the Board of GGC and the Board of DGG respectively. The Code of Conduct is set out in Annex 26.

To ensure DGG acts only on the delegated authority provided to it by GGC in the interest of GGC, DGG will set up four committees: (1) Asset and Liability Committee; (2) Impact Committee; (3) Credit Committee; and (4) Risk and Audit Committee) to provide oversight of all aspects of its operations. The committees will be responsible for ensuring that DGG's activities are carried out in line with operational and compliance, capital and liquidity, credit, market, and political risk. All committees will have terms of references which will be reviewed on an annual basis by the Board of GGC and the Board of DGG respectively. Further details of the four DGG Committees are set out in the Operations Manual (Operating Plan) (See Annex 21).

DGG will seek to monitor and control GGC's risk exposure through a variety of separately but complementary financial, credit, operational, liquidity, market, and political risk management tools and processes aligned with GGC’s Risk Management Framework. Further details of GGC’s Risk Management Framework are set out in Annex 26.

Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favor of disclosure for all information relating to the GCF, including its projects and programs. Therefore, any project or program documentation which is disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to the redaction of any information which may be considered confidential by the GGC Board, will be deemed to have been disclosed in accordance with the IDP. Such disclosure should not be applied to any one document if the document contains specific, segregable portions that can be disclosed separately.

Indicate below whether or not the funding proposal includes confidential information:

- ☐ No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.
- ☐ With confidential information: The accredited entity denies that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full. The accredited entity will provide a redacted version of this document accordingly, together with an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity's disclosure policy. This document should be provided to the Secretariat by the accredited entity.
- ☐ Redacted copy on disc: The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of two copies above, one containing confidential information.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP200

Proposal name: Green Guarantee Company

Accredited entity: MIFUG Bank Ltd.

Project/programme size: Large

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### Impact potential Scale: medium

1. There is indispensably an urgent need to scale up climate finance for developing countries to meet their Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) under the Paris Agreement. Green bonds and loans have proved to be an important option. The issuances of green bonds amounted to USD 20 billion in 2019, just over half of which was issued by developed countries. In 2020, however, just $8.9$ billion (or just 3 per cent) was issued in developing countries. In the past year, green bond issuances in developing countries have been static, reflecting not only the effects of the COVID-19 pandemic but also a lack of appetite for green bonds from investors due to risks in these countries.

2. There is an opportunity to attract global capital markets as, according to the World Bank, global investors allocate less than 2 per cent of their asset base to infrastructure investment in developing countries, which is insufficient to meet the climate finance gap. However, global institutions are not yet convinced that green bonds and loans are a suitable instrument for financing projects with environmental considerations.

3. Increasing green bond and loan issuance from developing countries represents a significant opportunity for attracting capital markets to invest in projects with environmental considerations. A green bond or loan can be defined as any type of bond or loan instrument where the proceeds will be exclusively applied to finance new and/or existing eligible projects, programmes, or activities that are environmentally sustainable.

4. However, as this assessment will indicate, not all green projects are climate projects, and differentiating between them is a challenge for investors and rating agencies alike.

5. One of the instruments that could attract capital markets to support climate finance is a guarantee. The Organization for Economic Co-operation and Development defines a guarantee as "a legal obligation by which one party agrees to pay or make good the amount due on a loan instrument in the event of non-payment by the borrower".

6. The Green Guarantee Company (GGC) will seek to provide guarantees to global investors to capitalise green bonds and loans for climate projects, particularly in developing countries. GGC is a newly created company established by three individual partners and Cardano Development Bank. It has been designed as an independent entity with no obligation of its founding partners in managing guarantees. Since the submission of the first funding proposal for the thirty-third meeting of the Board (B33) and the current proposal, GGC has changed one

<sup>1</sup> See <https://www.oecd.org/glossary/terms/93664.html>; <http://OECD-D2015stats.oecd.org/sds/#page=0&tab=2&n=282&e=>


---
**Page 93**
---

as of three individual founding members. The company will work with five international banks (origination partners) and rely on their infrastructure and standards to originate bond products, reduce execution risk and increase opportunities to scale at speed.

* The independent Technical Advisory Panel has concluded that there is a need to have guarantees in place for closing the real climate finance gap that exists in attracting international and national investors to support climate bonds and loans in developing countries. Moreover, the TAP has recommended that GGC should not be required to provide credit rating guarantees, owing to the lack of credit rating that only allows for a 1:1 leverage ratio on guarantees.

* The independent TAP evaluated the GGG proposal for B3.3 and provided the proponents with several comments on different aspects of the investment criteria. The proposals came back shortly thereafter with some changes in response to these comments, which addressed most of the concerns raised by the independent TAP but leaves other important concerns unresolved. If the Board decides that it is appropriate to proceed with the GGG proposal as presented, the understanding that this is a high-risk high-reward programme, with some of the risks related to these unresolved concerns raised by the independent TAP.

* The purpose of the GGC is to provide guarantees for bonds issued under the GGG-funded programme, as follows:

(a) Formation and capitalization of GGC: GGC will be formed as a private limited company in the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland and will be eventually capitalized with USD 250 million of equity. The equity will be raised in two tranches: the first tranche of USD 100 million to be raised within six months of operations and the second tranche of USD 150 million raised within 24 months to support the GGG programme. The company will be managed by a board of directors (DQG), also registered in the United Kingdom by the same partners, who will act as the manager, remaining independent from the DQG. There is no intention at this stage to establish any link between GGC and DQG which will outline the services that will be provided by the latter party on and when terms.

(b) Securing a triple A credit rating for GGC: the funding proposal states that GGC will need to secure an investment grade rating with any one of the three international credit rating agencies. The Board has been informed that there are several institutional investors in global credit and capital markets with the assurance that GGC is a creditworthy counterparty on whose guarantees they rely. Responding to a question on this matter, the DQG indicated that GGC has recently secured a triple A rating from Fitch.

Intended use of proceeds: The purpose of the GGG programme will be to attract institutional capital in global credit and capital markets to fund climate change mitigation and adaptation projects in developing countries. GGC will undertake the activities outlined above, including the origination of bonds and loans for climate change mitigation and adaptation projects which are aligned with the eligibility criteria outlined in the GGG proposal. The DQG will be responsible for identifying potential counterparties for and execute transactions in accordance with the guarantee process articulated in the Guarantee Credit Facility (GCF) document. The DQG will also be responsible for any transaction for which a guarantee could be provided against impact criteria, and the GGC guarantees will only be issued to projects that are aligned with the GGG sector). This screening of transactions should enable GGC to ensure that guarantees that they have issued under this programme will not be used in an inappropriate way (for example, to a general positive environmental impact).


---
**Page 94**
---

## Manage and report on portfolio of guarantees for risk management, performance and impact

This will include providing active risk management of the guarantee portfolio; providing support to borrowers in developing impact reporting frameworks; regular monitoring of the portfolio against agreed terms and conditions; environmental, social and governance issues and key climate performance indicators of borrowers; and ensuring that they can leverage more funds, including technical assistance funding to support borrowers.

### Establish commercial viability of GCC and facilitate exit for GCF, the Foreign Climate Facility (FCF), the European Investment Bank (EIB) and the United States Development Finance Corporation (DFC)

This involves assessing and developing a business case for each of these institutions to participate in the GCC. It is noted that the fund is expecting GCC to be part of GCF for 20 years but it may be possible for the exit to be made earlier.

### The Green Guarantee Company (GCC) will have a structure indicating how the GCC will work in the world, as seen in Figure 1, that needs to be revised in terms of countries involved, the inclusion of climate change reporting instead of ISO & ESG reporting and pricing.


| **Figure 1: How the Green Guarantee Company will work** |  |
| --- | --- |

| ![Image](https://example.com/image.png) |


### The most important point is to ensure that in each of the above-mentioned steps, there is a critical analysis of the climate change impacts. GCC will need to demonstrate how the loans and bonds for which guarantees are proposed to be provided expect to have a clear adaptation and/or mitigation strategy. In order to do this, the proponents must clearly understand and ensure climate impacts. GCC must retain a strong orientation towards the adaptation and mitigation strategy and not just focus on the financial aspects of the project; and critically, for thorough monitoring of results over time in terms of the GCF Integrated Results Management.


### The proponents included a list of eligible climate projects in annex 23 to the funding proposal—GCC Guarantee Policy. With a taxonomy that derived from the Climate Bonds Standard of the Green Bond Principles (GBP), the GCC will be able to identify which assets and projects can be used for climate bonds. From the GBP standards, the proponents included only five categories: (1) energy; (2) agriculture; (3) water; (4) buildings; and (5) waste and pollution control. The list is limited in scope as it does not include any criteria for a robust screening process and have limited indicators on greenhouse gas emissions and no adaptation indicators. Moreover, as explained in the paradigm shift section (section 1.2 below), GCC should


---
**Page 95**
---

12. The proponents have also provided a transaction selection process (annexes 2A to the funding proposal), involving five core elements: climate change adaptation, climate change mitigation, biodiversity conservation, ecosystem services and livelihoods, and gender equality (GES) aspects.

13. With the GHG Climate Bonds Standard as the primary standard in the transaction selection process, the independent TAP team see on issue with principle 5 (Mitigation Trade-Off Assessment): - "Climate mitigation requirements may be lowered for climate resilience focused assets or activities that are not expected to result in significant increases in greenhouse gas emissions and/or GHG emissions in the event of a disaster." Principle 5 indicates that a potential adaptation project can be considered as a mitigation project if it is expected to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. Even though principle 5 is correct, the independent TAP would recommend making sure that the transaction selection process does not allow projects with significant increases in emissions; relevant mitigation measures are implemented for maximum avoidance of increase or limitation of such emissions.

14. The following steps indicate that the process of ensuring climate change impacts will have checks and balances in each of the steps. As presented in figure 2, the process involves: (1) organizational review and approval by the borrower’s management team; (2) preparation of documentation; (3) review and challenge, in which the screening note is assessed and it is determined whether all seven requirements are met by in-house and external specialists as well as at the GCI; (4) independent TAP assessment, which will be conducted by independent technical specialists of the Impact Team and in-country specialists, and will result in a detailed transaction due diligence report, including an evaluation of the project’s climate change impacts under the GES and CBI standards; (5) remediation and disclosure, in which the DGG Impact Team under the guidance of the Independent TAP team will develop a remediation strategy to address the gaps/deficiencies of the project. The borrower is required to commit to delivering on this strategy within 12 months from the date of the transaction. If the borrower does not commit or expresses a clear reluctance to develop or deliver the remediation strategy; (6) monitoring and evaluation strategy developed by the GCI Impact Team, which will be reviewed by the Independent TAP team in consultation with the borrower. This is managed in parallel with the remediation step, ensuring a clear commitment by the borrower to deliver on this strategy within 12 months from the date of the transaction scored; detailed transaction Due Diligence report, remediation strategy and monitoring and evaluation strategy will be reviewed by the Independent TAP team and the GCI Committee, which will in turn review the commercial and financial aspects to issue a formal approval. If the Impact Committee has residual concerns, the credit team will be asked to go back to step 5.

15. It remains of concern as to whether the independent TAP team in the transaction selection process, those same individuals who are expected to assess the climate change impacts of projects. Moreover, it seems that the climate experts who form part of the DGG Impact Team could end up holding a conflict of interest with respect to the transaction selection process and the proponents in improving their projects while at the same time being responsible for approving or rejecting a project. Additionally, the independent TAP fully recognizes the importance of a remediation strategy on local level and the need to ensure that it is implemented. However, there are concerns that criteria for rejecting a project are not well clear and not well formulated. On the other hand, the DGG Impact Team has the responsibility to ensure that projects with a significant climate change added value will not be rejected owing to weak financial components and


---
**Page 96**
---

# Figure 2: Transaction process

| Step | Description |
| --- | --- |
| **Step A** | Initial due diligence questions by GCF Independent Evaluation Unit (IEU) |
| **Step B** | Review & challenge of initial screening questions by GCF Independent Evaluation Unit (IEU) |
| **Step C** | Final due diligence questions by GCF Independent Evaluation Unit (IEU) |
| **Step D** | Monitoring & evaluation of project performance by GCF Independent Evaluation Unit (IEU) |

The proponents included the details of the transaction scorecard in annexes 24B to the funding proposal. The scorecard has an initial screening with four main due diligence questions that reveal whether the project is eligible for funding. The first two questions are the ones that must be allocated different from the ones in the GCF Guarantee Policy. The second question requires a review of the project’s climate change mitigation and adaptation scores, which reflect screening demand. The scorecard covers the climate change mitigation and adaptation scores with the Rio markers. In addition to ensuring alignment with the Rio markers for climate change, the scorecard is consistent. CBI provides sector eligibility criteria in line with the GCF funding policy. The project must be aligned with the Rio markers, developed by the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development, screen climate projects for impacts on carbon fluxes and flows. A non-independent TAP believes that the project proponents should define their own initial eligibility criteria, which are different from their own definitions and their main interests, based on the market analysis of the eligible sectors in the socio-economic context.

In terms of expected adaptation and mitigation impacts, the proponents selected a portfolio approach with reference projects. For mitigation projects, the proponents used greenhouse gas (GHG) emission estimates for each project. The GCF Independent Evaluation Panel on Climate Change (2006, with the 2021 addition) and the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) have set a global target of limiting the net mitigation impact of 74 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (Mt CO₂ eq). However, since the real-world projects are not yet implemented, it is important to note that the estimates for the investment projects have been undertaken. It is vital that GCF tracks its impacts both to inform and to improve the ongoing screening process and to report back on this impact via MIFDC to GCF.

For adaptation projects, the proponents presented a rough estimate of potential direct and indirect benefits in each project. The GCF has identified 10 main priority sectors for adaptation result area sections according to the country’s NDC priorities (namely health, food, water security, disaster risk management, etc.). The estimates are based on a disaggregation of 50-50. In the absence of concrete projects, the estimates of 16,295,056 direct beneficiaries and 184,300 indirect beneficiaries over a 20-year time frame seem high.


---
**Page 97**
---

### In general, the transaction scorecard is on the right track of screening potential mitigation and adaptation transactions but will need ongoing refinement of the currently somewhat simplistic scoring criteria to ensure that it provides sufficient guidance to good decisions on allocations of guarantee funding to large adaptation and mitigation investments.

It is important to thoroughly understand the climate change component of a project. For example, a project may be considered as having a high level of vulnerability to climate change but the investment is that which wholly unrelated to climate change. It is shown to be part of a city's strategy to shape its urban development in a way that will reduce flood risk and improve water quality in the catchment are receiving, and are predicted to continue to receive, a much higher volume of rain per 24-hour period on peak days in comparison with a historical baseline period, with the changing climate. The project is not expected to have any significant impact on capacity to adapt.

The proponents added a new table section in article 1.45 of the amended funding proposal with potential programme interventions per country, very much concentrated in the water sector (flood and drought control, stormwater management and drainage infrastructure, irrigation, etc.). As it is known that climate change will have an impact on all sectors, the proponents included references to lessons, technologies and approaches relevant in particular national and subnational contexts. Each of the potential programme interventions is accompanied by a description of the expected impacts and the type of investment that GCF will seek to guarantee. Ongoing refinement of the screening process should be reported back through MUSG to GCF as part of the annual performance reviews.

The independent TAP notes that the overall governance structure now includes climate experts in the country team, but there is still a need for more involvement with members who are climate specialists. The proposal seems to have a better balance between involving project originators and other stakeholders such as national and subnational climate change experts than that presented in the original funding proposal. However, as expressed in the country team discussion, the proposal needs to refine the overall governance structure and checks and balances.

This is especially true when analysing the role of MUSG as the AE. In the proposed structure, the AE will be responsible for screening projects (as opposed to project originators [as expressed in the country ownership section]) to screen for and ensure climate impacts. Moreover, MUSG could have a more engaged role in strengthening the climate change aspects of the project.

Aside from this, there is no indication that GCF has considered how it will assess the potential for climate change adaptation and mitigation with prospective loans and bonds. The proponents commented that GCF will not be reliant on its origination partners to assess


---
**Page 98**
---

potential for climate change adaptation or mitigation and will be doing this in-house. To ensure a high-quality pipeline, however, the independent TAP believes that the originators should also have the capacity to screen and originate climate transactions, and that GCF would be strengthened by having a dedicated team of originators. In addition, the programme will need to undertake ongoing efforts to add to the pool of originators, including originators in developing countries. The pipeline of projects and transactions needs to be managed, if any of the originator partners receive media attention over allegations of greenwashing or other issues.

Moreover, since GCF will hire consultants at the national level, it would be important to establish partnerships in advance with specialized climate change entities in each of the countries that are potential originators. These entities should have a good understanding of what they could work with, but these are mostly from the global North. Enormous value can be added by supporting the capacity in the participating developing countries with local consultants who know their own markets and can help to identify opportunities for transactions. This would also give developing country originators the confidence that they have a partner in the proposed GCF countries.

27. The independent TAP believes that the proponents need to continue developing robust climate change screening criteria and an assessment process, ensuring the upfront capacity of the originator partners to screen projects and transactions. This could include the establishment of arrangements with relevant consultants or entities, both at the central company level of the originators as well as in-country, to support the development of the pipeline for the proposed GCF project.

28. The funding proposal has a weak baseline assessment of the climate finance needs per country. There is a risk of (i) failure to assess correctly the most appropriate financial product or service to be offered and (ii) failure to prioritize the urgent needs most urgent need access for finance for the most vulnerable countries. This is because the GCF has not yet developed a methodology for the climate finance assessments which the independent TAP has been requesting, it is difficult to know what the baseline assessment of the climate finance needs per country should be. In some cases, which have already issued green bonds (i.e., Brazil, Indonesia and Philippines), in some cases, the target GCF countries are not yet at a stage where they need or can access international capital as needed, since these countries already have investment grade rating (Philippines is already BBB+/BBB; Indonesia is BB+ and Brazil is B/B). The GCF guarantee may be best used for the countries that urgently need to access finance. In other cases, such as India, where there are no green bonds, this funding proposal needs a more in-depth assessment of the target countries' finance and investment sector.

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: medium

30. Developing guarantees is not new to the financial markets; however, there could be a paradigm shift by leveraging the use of guarantees provided to private-sector investors in the participating developing countries to accelerate climate change adaptation and mitigation finance, including through the mobilization of public and private sector capital. The use of guarantees to mobilize finance, if implemented successfully, has the potential to outstrip the mobilization impact of other less efficient interventions (grant funding).

# GCF (R.34/02)/MAB/R7 (Rev.01)

## Concessional debt etc.). Guarantees can maximize the climate finance impact of concessional funds while maintaining sufficient flexibility to adjust to changing markets and sectors to avoid over-subsidization.

### 35. GCF, as much so as it first ever guarantee company to exist in the global credit and capital markets with the sole objective of providing guarantees for climate change bonds and loans in developing markets. However, it is important to remember that several financial entities with an explicit mandate to provide guarantees for climate change investments have already existed around the world and could expand their facilities to guarantee climate change investments.

#### 36. The independent TAP ensures against the need to create a special vehicle to provide guarantees for climate change bonds and loans, as it can be used in conjunction with vehicles with different AIs in concrete and tangible climate change projects. Both the Secretariat and the Independent TAP are able to guarantee the full value of the portfolio it is guaranteeing. This means that GCF can provide a 1:1 leverage ratio on guarantees for climate change bonds and loans, which means that GCF could leverage up to ten times its equity for its portfolio of climate bonds.

#### 37. Notwithstanding the positive potential of guarantees, the Independent TAP notes that this project does not address the specific financial sector issues or the policy restrictions available in the selected NCI countries. With the very broad spread of types of economies targeted, it is difficult to identify a single set of best practices for all NCI countries. Developing countries like Brazil, India, Indonesia and the Philippines with advanced financial institutions and comparative experience are likely to have different needs than those of less developed financial markets such as Democratic Republic and Trinidad and Tobago with less developed financial markets does not make obvious sense or lend itself to the development of a coherent portfolio. In the view of the independent TAP, it is important that GCF should consider the regulatory and institutional frameworks in place and the opportunities to develop a strong climate finance market in each country before deciding on the most appropriate form of investment. Moreover, it would propose that going forward the GCF consider approaches guaranteeing bonds and loans for projects with a high level of co-financing. The Independent TAP also calls out more in-depth analysis, and possibly issue requests for proposals to identify a range of potentially suitable instruments in this space that could effectively leverage GCF funds for climate finance guarantee products.

#### 38. As explained in the impact potential section (section I.1 above), one of the biggest challenges facing the GCF is the lack of uniformity and clarity around the definitions of what is considered to be a green project, as well as the lack of internationally recognized methodologies and reporting taxonomies that are starting to be used by the financial investors. In the case of green bonds, some institutions have developed Green Bond Principles reflecting those developed by the International Capital Market Association (ICMA) for conventional bonds. These principles cover: (i.e. what eligible green projects will be financed); (ii) process for project evaluation and selection; (iii) reporting on the use of proceeds.

#### 39. However, even though there could be 'second-party opinions' with entities that investigate the purpose of the investments and verify the use of proceeds), it is very difficult for counterparties to ensure that the green bond market is not being used as a vehicle to avoid double accounting. Moreover, several green bond issuances have refinanced existing projects, which has led to some confusion about whether these are truly new investments or just re-packaging of existing ones.

#### 40. Therefore, instead of just continuing to follow standard procedures, which does not create a paradigm shift, the proponents should aim to guarantee climate bond issuance that prove their impact, and ensure that the GCF is able to verify this impact. This would also help to identify the real investments behind the climate bonds, and bring additionality in terms of new projects. Otherwise, there could be a potential risk of greenwashing.


---
**Page 100**
---

### In This Regard, The Proponents Explain That GCF Will Focus on Supporting New Capital Expenditures in Relation to Climate Projects but May Consider a Refinancing, Subject to Two Conditions Being Met: (1) the Refinancing Is a Component of a Larger Arrangement that Includes New Capital Expenditures; and (2) the Refinancing Is Mobilizing Global Institutional Investors That Would Not Otherwise Have Invested in the Project. The Proponents Also Emphasize That, as Stated Above, More than Just Mobilizing Capital Is the Necessity of Ensuring that the Underlying Project Has a Realistic Chance of Success.

#### As Explained in the Climate Impact Potential Section, the Proponents Will Rely Solely on the CBI Standard; Having Carried Out Research and Analysis of the Different Existing Standards. However, They Are Aware That There Is No Single Standard for All Countries, and There Are Very Good Standards Like the European Union and Asian Standards that Could Be Used by Investors, or It May be Possible to Adapt Some of Such Standards Depending on the Host/Developing Country. Access is Only the Entry Point to Screen Projects; The Underlying Assumption Will Be to Align the Standards with a Much More Robust Analysis Through the Transaction Selection Process.

#### On the Other Hand, the Project Presents a Theory of Change and Has Revised Its Impact Objective: "If the GCF Receive Equity Capital from the GCF, THEN Significant Private Sector Capital will be Mobilized." The Proponents Also Emphasize That the Project Will Ensure that Relevant Climate Knowledge Generation Will Be Transferred to Participating Countries, Providing Partnerships for Capacity Building. In Addition, the Project Will Provide a Mechanism for the GCF to Provide [a] De-Risking Mechanism in the Form of Credit Guarantees as Well as Associated Risk Management and Reporting and Due Diligence Processes to Ensure That the Private Sector Is Climate Finance.

#### This Is Not about Receiving Equity Capital from GCF, and Possibly Only to Provide a Service. The Project Clearly States that It Has a More-Lighted-Up Goal than Simply Receiving the Capital from GCF and Should Have the Vision to Maximize the Opportunity to Support Innovative and Effective Climate Change Projects. In Addition, the Proponents Are Very Clear That They Will Be Refrained to State That If GCF Is Able to Mobilize Global Capital Towards Climate Initiatives, Then Several Other Entities Will Also Be Able to Do So, Including Multilateral Development Banks and Other Climate Impacts, Ensuring Knowledge Transfer. The Whole Point of the Programme Is to Ensure Climate Impacts, Not Just to Mobilize Financial Capital Through Guarantees.

#### In Response to Questions Raised by the Independent TAP During the Question-and-Answer Session, the Proponents Assured That the Success of GCF Is Derived Mostly from the Experience and Expertise of the Three Partners. The Proponents Also Assured That This Project Is Not Solely Dependent on the Role and Experience of the Three Partners and Company Managers of GCF and IDFC; Rather It Should Include the Development and a Detailed Description of a More Robust Structure for the Project, Including the Involvement of a Larger Number of Entities Around the Structure, Including the Partner Organizers, Institutions That Could Technically Support the Project, and Other Financial Institutions. The Proponents Also Assured That There Are No Climate Finance Initiatives in the Selected Countries, as Well as the Investors.

#### Since B331, One of the Three Funding Members, Namely Jean-Pierre Sveens, Has Left the Company. The Proponents Also Assured That Both GCF and IDFC Are Both Start-Ups, Which by Definition Have No Track Record and No Solid Institutional Capacity. When the Independent TAP Raised Concerns About the Sustainability of the Project’s Financial Structure, the AE Responded that Cardano Development Had a Track Record and Could Back Up the Sustainability of the Project. The Proponents Also Assured That the GCF Should Not Be the Source of Initiatives; Rather It Should Be the Organization That Ensures the Stability of GCF. The Stability of the Company Becomes, in the Eyes of the Independent TAP, the Main Risk of This Transaction.

### Sustainable development potential: Scale: medium to high

#### Environmental and social co-benefits

The proponents have developed a transaction selection process in which they analyze the underlying project risks and opportunities for transactions to which the company needs to provide a credit guarantee. The approach is to measure the impacts on (i) climate change adaptation and climate change mitigation; (ii) climate certification; (iii) gender and social inclusion; and (iv) ILO standards.


---
**Page 102**
---

### Economic co-benefits

The programme is intended to increase access to guarantees and access to private capital for implementing climate change projects that otherwise could have difficulties in ensuring a financial return on investment. The proposal should include an explanation of how it will generate revenues for project developers, confidence of the private sector in developing climate change projects, sectoral innovation, reduction in prices of energy, water and transport in selected territories.

### Gender-sensitive development

The funding proposal provides a gender action plan with gender action principles that the project proponents must ensure are respected prior to the issuance of a guarantee and that should be included as part of the project design. The proposal should include a gender analysis or documents) as well as project implementation (e.g., reporting on progress).

A gender assessment was conducted in the absence of a pipeline project, and therefore the proposals explain how they are taking into account gender equality and women’s empowerment key developments.

This information explicitly provides a baseline of what GGCF will require with regard to the selected projects and results areas with respect to gender, including vulnerabilities and capacities for change. The findings from these sector and country assessments have been used to develop the GGCF Gender Action Plan, which sets out the principles that should be considered as part of the project design process to maximize the gender benefits of a specific project.

Basically, the assessment is a matrix showing challenges faced by women in the different sectors of the project, highlighting countries where the challenge may be significant or highly significant. The proposal must include an explanation of how it will address these challenges and should first present the country context, then the sectoral context and finally the potential impact on gender equality.

The assessment reflects the point of departure at which GGCF would require a project investor, developer or owner to provide a more detailed, context-specific gender analysis, an assessment of the gender impacts of the project and how these will be addressed. The gender deliberation will be reviewed during the seven steps of the transaction selection process, including a Board of Governors meeting as part of the screening process.

The proposal also acknowledges the challenges of working with women in areas as green bond issuers, including the lack of data, the lack of predictable modelling capabilities, the higher transaction costs of preparing gender plans and the perceived lack of urgency. In fact, few

## Brazil's Economic Performance

Brazil’s economic performance has been varied since the onset of the pandemic in the second half of 2020. After the devastating first wave of COVID-19, Brazil witnessed a rebound in GDP growth to 4% in Q3 and 5% in Q4 of 2020. However, this recovery was short-lived as the country experienced a second wave of infections in early 2021, leading to a sharp increase in inflation, which have dampened that recovery. Inflation and unemployment remain high and consumption has been weak. Brazil’s economy is expected to grow by only 3% in 2021, down from the country's gross domestic product in 2022 from 2.1 to 1.5 per cent. For 2023, the predicted 2.6 per cent growth rate is also below expectations.

It is worth mentioning that the conflict between the Russian Federation and Ukraine and its global economic ramifications have different implications for the different country economies. For example, Brazil’s economy has been negatively affected by the war in Ukraine as it accounts for 40 percent of total merchandise exports.

According to a reference given from the proposals from a report of the German Agency for International Cooperation (GIZ), Brazil issued its first green bond on November 2020, 10 green bonds; one transition bond and one sustainability-linked bond were placed in the foreign market, totalling USD 6.4 billion. Although Brazil has only issued four green bonds with a total amount of issuances (22 per cent), they have raised an amount of resources that is almost three times as large or double the amount of resources raised by all other countries combined. It is worth noting that in Brazil one in five green bonds was issued in a global capital market but mobilized three times more resources than those issued on domestic markets.

TAPES believes that it would be interesting to analyze the underlying green impacts of the bond issuances as a factor of success.


## Indonesia's Economic Performance

Indonesia is the world’s fourth most populous nation and tenth largest economy in terms of purchasing power parity. Indonesia’s economy is now recovering and the country’s gross domestic product (GDP) growth rate has been supported by growing commodity exports and accommodative fiscal policy to weather the pandemic.

Indonesia has issued 17 green, social and sustainability bonds to the value of USD 7.2 billion. The proceeds from these bond issuances will be used for renewable energy assets, namely PLN and Pertamina. These will be used to fund renewable energy asset. Moreover, Indonesia’s first sovereign green bond was issued by the Indonesian government in February 2018. It was the world's first sovereign bond exclusively aimed at funding climate change initiatives in a manner whereby financing is compliant with Islamic law.

The Philippines has been one of the fastest-growing economies in East Asia and the Pacific region. Average annual growth increased from 6 to 4 per cent between 2010 and 2019 from an average of 4.5 per cent between 2000 and 2009. With increasing urbanization, a growing middle class and rising population, the Philippines is expected to continue its strong consumer demand supported by a vibrant labour market and robust remittances. Growth contractions in 2018 and 2019 were due to external factors such as trade tensions but the economy has started to recover with a 5.6 per cent year-on-year expansion in 2021.

Commercial banks in the Philippines have issued around USD 29 billion worth of sustainable bonds since 2017. More recently, the Government of the Philippines announced the


---
**Page 105**
---

first green bond offer of USD 500 million to raise funds for clean energy projects to comply with its ambitious climate change target?71. Trinidad and Tobago is expected to grow in 2022, from a very broad-based economy. In the energy sector, higher gas and oil prices will support the growth, higher prices, particularly internationally, are expected to cause a decline in production, a change with specific pressure on prices of international agricultural commodities.72. Argentina and Brazil have also issued green bonds. The Philippines has a well-established financial system capable of issuing green bonds and loans on a large scale without the need for guarantees. Moreover, there are banks with good ratings in these economies able to originate green bonds. In addition, the Philippines is expected to grow in 2022.73. The proponents argue that even though there have been issuances of green bonds in the above mentioned countries, they have not yet reached a level where they can be considered as part of global capital markets. They explain that GCF serves the interest of a developing country by creating a pathway for climate projects in that country to access international investors that only operate on a global scale.74. However, in the large economies, GCF will be able to gradually guarantee some transactions and thus help to build confidence among investors about the long-term economic performance of the selected sectors in each of these countries. The countries have been able to make a difference in financing climate projects with their own sovereign issuances. On the other hand, it is argued that the risk associated with a climate project in a developing country is not materially higher than the risk associated with any other type of investment. The proponents argue this and simply assume that the risk is much higher. The proponents therefore argue that a guarantee from GCF will be necessary to attract investors.75. In terms of countries like the Lao People's Democratic Republic and Trinidad and Tobago, with smaller economies and less developed financial systems, GCF could make a difference in supporting climate projects. The proponents argue that the Lao PDR has not yet issued any comprehensive market analysis of the existing demand for guarantees in line with the selected criteria. In addition, there is no information on the potential for financing climate adaptation projects that could be guaranteed, with a special emphasis in the least developed NDC countries.76. In any event, the project proponents have not presented any analysis of the climate finance system in each of the selected countries, even though it was one of the main points of the independent review. The proponents also did not provide an assessment of the potential for increased resilience of the most vulnerable people and communities; health and well-being; food and water security; infrastructure and built environment; and ecosystems and ecosystem services. In addition, there is no information on how GCF could support further screening on this aspect, as well as on the need to ensure that there is no maladaptation.1.5 Country ownershipScale: low to medium

1.3.1 Alignment with national climate strategy

The proponents have involved seven countries that have issued NDCs, providing country climate commitments and reports, including analysis of the past, present and future climate, and explaining the climate commitments and enabling environments. They have carried out short desk reviews to assess the alignment between the project and the NDCs, as well as any relevant financial strategies or, any relevant documentation demonstrating that there has been engagement with relevant national authorities, such as ministries or with financial institutions to assess their interest in and need of the GCF guaranties.

The independent TAP noted that if GCF intends to stimulate financial markets in all the participating countries, it should consider engaging with investors from both public and private sector investments in climate change mitigation and adaptation in selected sectors, there should be a clear indication on how the project will contribute to these goals. The TAP also argued that investors are looking for very simple guarantees of the payment obligations of a climate project and this is what GCF will provide. It is important to note that the TAP has not been accepted as a GCF transaction, but it was accepted as a GCF transaction via the GCF-approved transaction selection process. It is very important to note that the TAP is not an indicative list of transactions in each country and not just any project claiming to have a climate related impact.

Moreover, there are national and international financial institutions with investment grades that issue guaranties in the selected countries that could be potentially interested in investigating the GCF transaction. The proponents should consider exploring the concept of GCF should be established, engaging national and international institutions interested in exploring the possibility of providing guarantees to the project. It is important to note that it is possible for a national guarantor to be rated investment grade on a local rating scale but not so as an international institution. Therefore, the proponents should consider the local rating and owns the guaranty fully and will support all its obligations. The proponents are not aware of national guarantors in developing countries that are providing guarantees in global climate related projects. It is important to note that there could be opportunities and pathways to combine efforts with national and international financial institutions.

It is recommended that GCF should engage with the selected countries through country engagement process, analyzing in depth the alignment with national climate finance procedures and strategies which GCF could support. Establishing a working group in each of the countries would be an appropriate way for GCF to engage with the relevant authorities in the programme to establish the willingness of the relevant entities in each country to develop such an arrangement. The TAP is an indicative list of potential projects and national working group that could support their effort.

In most countries strategies are being put in place by relevant institutions to accelerate climate change mitigation and adaptation from direct conversations with the GCF proponents, in order to align purposes and to gain support in the origination of climate change transactions. Therefore, it is recommended that GCF should propose a project implementation and national working group in each of the selected countries to ensure real country engagement.

1.3.2 Capacity of accredited entities and executing entities to deliver

The proposal is structured under a complex set of arrangements between GCF, MIFUG, HGC and DGG. The funding document will be prepared by the proponents for submission to AEI with an overview on how the project implementation and will ensure project compliance with the relevant MIFUG policies as well as from the perspectives of GCF interest in the project. MIFUG will execute a subsidiary agreement (implementation agreement) with the executing entities (EEs)


---
**Page 107**
---

(DGC, GCF Holdings and GCF Holdings Manager) and this agreement passes down the GCF requirements relevant to execution for the recruiting entities. In line with the legal arrangements, the AE will remain responsible for all the oversight and supervision obligations of the AE under the recruitment process.

### In order for GCF not to be a direct shareholder of GCC, MUFIC will incorporate a special purpose company (GCF Holdings) along with a Holdings Manager. Both GCF Holdings and the Holdings Manager are wholly owned by MUFIC.

#### MUFIC highlighted its role in putting in place the legal agreements with DGCs and GGC and in acting as a partner originator but did not provide a clear sense of how it would apply its capacity to ensure that the AE plays an active role. The TAP team has raised concerns about this fundamental question from the independent TAP having been weak, leaving the impression that the AE will play no meaningful role.

#### According to the proponents, MUFIC will hire an external legal counsel who has reviewed past GCF transactions by MUFIC (PTF15 and PTF22) and will ensure that only the appropriate clauses are included in the agreements. The TAP team is concerned about the adequacy of this for the fulfillment of the contractual obligations of EAs. A clear assurance that the AE will play the full role described above would be required to address these concerns.

#### MUFIC will assign a qualified individual on behalf of GCF as a shareholder of GCC. However, this individual will not sit on the GCC Board of Directors and will not have any voting rights in relation to the GCC Board of Directors. This individual is based in London; he will be one of the originating partners for GCC. It is the view of the independent TAP that this arrangement does not provide sufficient assurance that the AE will play an active role, since the division of MUFIC that is originating deals will aim to maximize their guarantee coverage, while the division of MUFIC that is playing the oversight role aims to review all deals critically, rejecting those that are not in line with the GCF’s interest. The TAP team has raised concerns about any misconception of an AE simply being used as a “pass-through” vehicle to enable other institutions to access GCC.

#### The funding proposal also points out that MUFIC is in discussion with its affiliate company to become the sole shareholder of GCC. The TAP team has raised further questions on how this will address further questions about how the various roles of MUFIC will be balanced, and conflict of interest avoided.

### In response to the concerns of the independent TAP, the proponents responded that they understand that a member of the GCF Board of Directors and a major shareholder of GCC, MUFIC may not be able to play an active role in the oversight function for GCC. They have proposed, for example as a deal originator. They say that MUFIC has an internal policy for managing conflicts of interest and that this will be applied to any situation where there is a conflict of interest. This regard would be minimized, since the GCF Emerging Markets Team is based in London and there is no overlap with the GCC Board (e.g., different staff, files, etc.) between different divisions within MUFIC, with a strict firewall maintained.

### However, in the view of the independent TAP team, this issue is not about sharing information systems. The issue is about the structure set up to ensure that the AE plays an active role in relation to the EAs (GCC overall governance) and the originating partners, which should be clearly distinct and defined within the structure set up in order to maintain the effectiveness of the GCF investment.

### Both MUFIC and its affiliate company are established in the United Kingdom. Since neither of these companies has a track record, the proponents provided the resumes of the shareholders and founding members of the companies.


---
**Page 108**

The first funding proposal stated that DGG Ltd. was founded by Bao Hieu Khan, Jean Pierre Sweerts and Lea Luisita Pereira and incorporated at Companies House (United Kingdom) in February 2021, with Luisita Pereira owning 75 per cent of the shares. It was noted by the independent TAP that the Board of Directors had been replaced by new partners Bao Hieu Khan and Jean Pierre Sweerts. The AE reassured the independent TAP of Mr Peter Coates that the Board of Directors would be replaced with new partners, including Luisita Pereira, a subsidiary of Cardano Development which had the contract to manage Guarantees. However, there is concern to the independent TAP that none of the three current partners has a strong background in environmental matters or climate finance, as was the case with Mr Sweerts.

Moreover, the proposal stated that the governance structure of DGG Ltd., and by extension the sustainability of GGC, depends on the small group of partners, any one of whom could at any point decide to leave the company. Through its joint call with the independent TAP, the AE noted that the status quo was the giving of Cardano Development a 20 per cent stake in DGG Ltd., as an incubator and fund manager, which supports its scale-up funds and a number of start-up with ongoing management services. The proposal stated that the ownership structure of DGG will evolve away from financial close to be 60 per cent owned by the management team and 40 per cent owned by Cardano Development. However, it is noted that through Cardano Development, DGG will be the manager running the day-to-day operations, with a management team of four directors.

91. Cardano Development provides DGG with governance oversight (it will be the Chair of the Board), risk management services (it will be present on all DGG internal committees) and operational support (it will provide DGG with access to its network of experts). It also has a network in developing countries. Cardano Development will also have one representative who sits on the DGG Board, as well as a representative on the DGG Risk and Audit Committee, bringing their own significant emerging market investment and risk management experience to the table.

92. As noted by the TAP, there is a risk to GGC in accepting the past experience in the financial sector of the three individual founding partners of DGG Ltd., an assurance of success. First, because compositionally, the Board of Directors will be made up entirely of individuals who have not previously not responded to the purpose of the company or its long-term commitments. Second, because GGC is highly dependent on Cardano Development to support the founding members, the overall governance structure of DGG Ltd., and by extension the sustainability of GGC, depends on the AE. Third, there is no individual funding member with experience in climate change, and again the recipients will be unable to provide any assurance that they have the necessary expertise.

93. In terms of the GGC governance, the proposal stated that the role of the Board of Directors will be to review, challenge and approve the annual strategic budget for DGG proposed by the Manager of DGG Ltd., as well as the annual financial statements and the annual operating plan and budget. Four of these directors are to be appointed by the holders of the ordinary shares with the remaining four directors being appointed by the AE. The Board will meet at least once a year, but may also meet more frequently if required. There will be a five-member Impact Committee comprising independent climate change adaptation, climate finance and development experts who will provide advice on projects to the Board and the Committee also acting as advisors to the GGC Board. There will also be an Investment Committee or Credit Committee which will review all investment proposals, including financial, environmental, social, financial and commercial aspects of the transactions and approve the transactions that have been first approved by the Impact Committee.

94. The TAP expressed concern on the previous version of the funding proposal that the rules of the Board seemed very detached from the most important objective of GGC and DGG, which is to guarantee effective climate change projects, and was concerned about the fact that the Board will have no veto or decision-making power over the approval of the guarantees, leaving the
