### Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders

The project proposal has not included concrete evidence of consultations in the countries, involving not only NGOs but all relevant stakeholders in the climate finance space in each of the countries.

The proponents explain that engagement with stakeholders in a target country will be a continuous and ongoing process for GGC and that guarantee transactions will be undertaken in consultation with the relevant stakeholders. The proponents have engaged with stakeholders in Nigeria and country programs.

To date, GGC, in partnership with AS-AC, MIFRC has begun consultations with Bangladesh, Brazil, Cambodia, India, Indonesia, Kenya, the Lao People's Democratic Republic, the Philippines, Rwanda, South Africa, Trinidad and Tobago, and Uganda. The proponents explain that they will engage with stakeholders in each of these countries alongside the NDA. However, the outcomes of these consultations are not presented in the funding proposal.

The proponents explain that in each of the transactions, they will engage with local stakeholders, including vulnerable communities (e.g., indigenous peoples) and vulnerable groups, for a working group to be formed. In addition to engaging with national-level representatives from the NDA and other relevant parties, GGC will seek to engage in-country consultants where necessary to help it to communicate effectively with these stakeholders, including non-governmental organizations and civil society organizations where appropriate.

The proponents commit to developing a grievance mechanism and to formally address complaints or concerns raised by stakeholders. They explain that this will be done through established advertising procedures, and taking into consideration cultural and language differences.

In the stakeholder engagement plan in the proposal, GGC proposes to establish a working group of national-level representatives from the NDA and other relevant parties where any interventions are complementary to the countries' existing approved and pipeline projects, as well as to ensure that GGC's activities complement those being undertaken by other donors in these countries, to ensure this complementarity. They explain that the working group will be a forum for GGC to engage with stakeholders on its proposals, to better understand their needs for local expertise and capital markets, and to solicit feedback and make improvements. However, there is no explanation of how GGC will identify or engage with these national-level representatives responsible persons, budget and agreements in their proposed roles in the different countries.

The independent TAG had already suggested in the previous assessment that GGC should develop a stakeholder engagement plan for each country where it proposes to submit its funding proposal to GCF, ensuring that the proposal is aligned with current climate finance strategies and needs in each country, as well as regulatory frameworks and opportunities to provide

1. **Efficiency and effectiveness** Scale: medium

Cost-effectiveness and cost-efficiency

The programme will use USD 82.5 million from GCF equity up to a rolling portfolio of USD 4 billion or 40 times leverage. GGC expects to leverage the first tranche of USD 100 million of equity into a total guarantee capacity of USD 6 billion (USD 100 million x 60 x 10), which, multiplied by 10, could create an initial guarantee capacity of USD 8 billion.

The GGC business plan leverages environment USD 400 million of capital (split between USD 250 million from the World Bank and USD 150 million from other sources) to provide guarantees to be raised later) to provide guarantees to mobilize USD 8 billion of green bonds and loans from global credit and capital markets for climate finance in developing countries over a 10-year period.

For GCF, it has been established that a leverage multiple of 10 will allow it to maintain an investment grade rating. The business plan proposes a rolling portfolio of USD 4 billion or 60 times guarantee capacity. GGC will provide guarantees at scale - USD 50 million to USD 200 million per project. GGC expects to have the ability to raise USD 8 billion in total over its expected 10-year lifetime of the business plan, to meet its objective of building a USD 6 billion portfolio by year 10.

Overall, USD 82.5 million of GCF funding will deliver mitigation benefits at USD 1.9 per t CO2 eq (USD 37.5 per tonne).

According to the proponents, if taking into account resolving use of GCF equity contribution to support a revolving guarantee portfolio (maturing and/or amortized guarantees recycled back into new projects), GGC could provide USD 4 billion in total leverage over the first ten years. From this 22.5 percent per year increase in total guarantees issued against the USD 6 million guarantee capacity on a rolling basis, GGC has the potential to deliver mitigation benefits at USD 1.99 per t CO2 eq (USD 39.8 per tonne), expected lifetime emission reductions based on total guarantees issued (4.14 million t).

Despite this, the proponents believe that GGC will still deliver climate change mitigation benefits at an estimated USD 892.9 per t CO2 eq (total project costs of USD 5.71 billion if USD is USD 6 billion/expected lifetime emission reductions), based on the same assumptions and calculations as above.

If calculated on total guarantee issued basis of USD 4.9 billion, the total investment costs of benefits ratio would be USD 803 per t CO2 eq (USD 5.71 billion/total guarantees issued).

Little detail was provided, however, in annexes to the funding proposal with the climate impact calculations on the rationale behind the assumptions and calculations. The independent TAP noted that these figures are based on a number of assumptions and should be considered as rough estimate.

GGC will guarantee both listed and unlisted debt, allowing flexibility to mobilize global investors to provide climate finance. GGC will work with international and regional banks that have a licence to arrange bonds and loans and sell them to global investors (which is a regulated activity) and other financial institutions. The TAP notes that GGC may also collaborate possibly with others, that will use their networks in the target countries to identify suitable climate change mitigation and adaptation transactions. The independent TAP believes that there


---
**Page 112**
---

should be a stronger connection with the national institutions supporting the acceleration of climate finance in the selected countries. Moreover, it is crucial that the due diligence efforts look at the broader market risks associated with the enabling environment of the relevant country.

127. GCF is expected to be a self-sustaining programme with no defined lifespan, but it is proposed that the fund should have an exit strategy. In response to the independent TAP about the rationale for this time frame and the exit strategy, the proponents explained that the exit strategy is not yet fully developed as it depends on the market conditions (either through a listing on a stock exchange or sale to a private sector third party) as soon as possible. The proponents also noted that they are currently in discussions with the World Bank Group on whether this is achievable in a shorter term or whether there are other exit strategies such as some form of debt instrument.

128. Guarantees will be exercised only if they are needed. Once the guarantee process has been completed and a guarantee has been issued, there is no immediate flow of funds from GCF but rather from committed investors to the borrower via a debt instrument, as would occur in a conventional financing.

129. Since guarantees are unpredictable and ad hoc in nature, GCF cash equity is invested in highly liquid assets (e.g., US Treasuries) or in a trust account at the World Bank Group (e.g., Pacific Investment Management Company) on behalf of GCF. This allows GCF to earn income on these investments which contributes to covering its operating expenses as it builds its guarantee portfolio.

128. The proponents explain that originator partners’ profit motive means that they cannot invest their own capital in the transaction structure and therefore, the existence of a guarantor from GCF, global investors will be more comfortable entering into a transaction on the basis that there is an additional layer to the transaction structure and expressed its comfort by choosing to provide the guarantee.

130. The GCF default reserve continues two aspects. First, GCF will primarily guarantee senior debt and be exposed to less financial risk than the full amount of debt made available to senior lenders. GCF will also control the investors’ ability to accelerate a loan by having the right to call on the borrower to repay its debt in full if it defaults on a debt instrument. This ensures that GCF can protect its own liquidity position while creating sufficient time as well as resources for the originator partners to determine how best to protect its position and recover any capital that has been put out under a guarantee.

132. On the other hand, GCF will also typically have a recourse agreement with the borrower in which it is agreed that GCF will be able to take action on behalf of the borrower if it is aware of a potential default well in advance of the default happening and has the ability to take action before the borrower’s position. In the view of the independent TAP, GCF can use public resources and will therefore have more flexibility than originator partners in dealing with all relevant parties.

134. However, the performance fee for the overall costs of creating a specialized company to provide guarantees, this seems very costly. In the first funding proposal DGG was proposing to apply a fee cap based on a percentage of portfolio. Fifty per cent of the fee cap is payable as a third fee to cover the cost of the originator partners’ profit motive and fifty per cent covers variable costs related to overheads, third-party consultants and other costs such as regulatory costs etc.

124. In response to the independent TAP remark on the costs, the AE clarified that it understands that DGG fees include several costs which would typically be covered by the fund and not included in the fee cap. The AE also explained that these costs are related to transaction work (e.g., in-country climate, gender and/or EKS consultants). These costs had originally been included in the management fee to simplify the cost structure of GCF.


---
**Page 113**
---

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The independent TAP recognizes the value of this transaction aiming to enable GCF as a shareholder of a newly created guarantee company (i.e., GGc), to potentially leverage its equity notional and credit rating to access the capital markets. This would allow GCF to provide guarantees at lower costs than what would be possible via direct investment models.

### II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The independent TAP recognizes the value of this transaction aiming to enable GCF as a shareholder of a newly created guarantee company (i.e., GGc), to potentially leverage its equity notional and credit rating to access the capital markets. This would allow GCF to provide guarantees at lower costs than what would be possible via direct investment models.


---
**Page 114**
---

108. The Board has been asking the Secretariat for several years to seek alternative models for guarantees to support climate finance. The concept is important and conceptually worthy of GCF funding and support. However, the Secretariat has never put together a request for proposal to receive feedback on the concept. The independent TAP sees this as an opportunity to provide feedback to the Board. The independent TAP sees a mixed opportunity. The current proposal provides one alternative, but GCF could have become involved in other areas of work that are not covered by this proposal.

109. As explained in the first section of the assessment, the independent TAP evaluated the GCF proposal for B3 and provided the proposals with several comments on different aspects of the proposal. The independent TAP has also reviewed the revised proposal and is now submitting a revised proposal to be submitted to B3.8, resolving some of the concerns observed by the independent TAP but not all.

110. Climate impact: the core purpose of GCF is to support interventions that have a clear climate purpose and result in beneficial climate impacts, whether mitigation or adaptation. The proposed project does not meet this criterion as it does not clearly describe its screening criteria and overall proposed methodology to assess the climate impacts of the loans and bonds that will be issued under the transaction. The independent TAP recommends revising these screening criteria, avoiding the potential confusion by relying on standards and taxonomies from other entities (e.g., CBI, the contribution of Working Group II to the Fifth Assessment Report of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change). The independent TAP also suggests that the proponents could further refine the process, as the definitions in the screening criteria are too ambiguous. This would help to ensure alignment with GCF’s climate focus and support adaptation and mitigation investments. Special attention should be given to adaptation project screening criteria when it is possible to analyze climate impacts of projects or specific interventions for resilience.

111. Additionally, the independent TAP noted that the project proponents develop their dialogue with the transaction partners, ensuring that they have the capacity and knowledge to support the operation of the transaction. The proponents resolved this issue, explaining how they will ensure that the transaction partners are well informed about GCF so that they can effectively assess what is a suitable transaction to introduce to GCF. The independent TAP also noted that the proponents have demonstrated their ability to leverage their real expertise on climate change capacity, and recognized experience in working in the selected geographies.

112. Adaptation and governance: the revised proposal has made considerable advances in proposing the involvement of significant numbers of role players in key positions, including the GCF Board, the Independent Evaluation Office, and the Secretariat. This is a positive step towards critical climate change analysis. In response to the suggestion of the independent TAP to have a Board with a veto on the transactions, the proponents agreed to have three knowledgeable Board members who will be able to provide advice in the areas of adaptation, resilience, and Gender Committees, with veto power. However, the final governance structure needs to be further revised and clarified.

113. One of the main concerns of the independent TAP is that GGC and DGG are both start-ups, which by definition have no track record. GGC is trying to demonstrate the capacity to achieve its goal on climate change adaptation in a very short time frame. The independent TAP found this development. However, since the independent TAP evaluated the first assessment, one of the founding members of GGC has left the organization and another member has resigned, demonstrating that this could continue to happen at any point. Moreover, the stability of the


---
**Page 115**
---

company is heavily dependent on one individual founding member who owns 70 per cent of the shares of DGG and on Cardano Development, posing risks for GCF investment.

Although it is difficult to establish a clause of permanence, GCF should be able to decide on the legal procedures to ensure that the partners of the GCF commit to sustaining the company, and if they do not, then the GCF can take over the company. The TAP has been working with Cardano Development on the permanence or exit strategy of the structure. The independent TAP also recommends that the GCF should review its agreements with DGG and GCG considering the concentrated ownership structure of GGG and its main funding source.

The independent TAP remains concerned about the role of MUIF as AE, which will have only one seat on the Board of Directors and has not described a clear process through which it will apply for or receive any information from the GCF. In addition to the lack of transparency that will originate transactions, MUIF is putting itself in a situation of potential conflict of interest that needs to be addressed.

The GCF legal team should draft a firewall policy and governance procedures on improper use of, sharing and discussing material and non-public information within GGG, especially between the Board members and the investment decision makers (i.e. Credit Committee, GCG Board), and between MUIF as AE, DGG as AE and GCG as AE.

Country ownership: as the programme evolves, the proponents need to develop a much stronger analysis of the market, policies and institutional capacities to support guarantees and general financial instruments in each country. The TAP recommends that a more detailed risk analysis for each of the countries will which must deepen. It is critical to ensure a real engagement with the governments and other stakeholders in each country, including those involved in supporting climate guarantees and with the climate finance actors that could provide support. Country ownership and engagement with the GCF and its partners are also important but are only one point of entry to a much stronger analysis of the capacities and needs within countries to use guarantees to support their NDC goals, and engagement with country institutions.

In offering GCF products and services to NOL countries, GCG should prioritize those that have no or limited access to financial markets. A specific risk assessment and mitigation approach should be developed. For example, a weighted percentage of the countries' population to the total population benefiting from the GCF product, whichever is appropriate, as this reference point will help determine which countries are more attractive for investment and less attractive countries will be able to secure much-needed climate finance in support of their NDC commitments.

Cost-effectiveness: if guarantees are to be proved to work, it is critical that they should be available at an affordable cost. In the end, guarantees will only be used in the case of default by the borrower. The TAP recommends that GCF consider using a third-party guarantee structure such as Pacific Investment Management Company until it is needed. The overall structure and costs associated with the guarantee model are very high due to the administrative fees and transactional costs and overheads all the way from the origination to actual delivery of the guarantee, which could be prohibitive for some countries. In addition, there may be other potential investors could be subject to risk in order to prove the guarantee model, but it is also important that GCF should consider the cost structure of the guarantee model and how this cost structure of the project promotes maximum cost-effectiveness. As the independent TAP has not been able to review the final budget and fee schedule it is difficult to ascertain whether the requested guarantees are affordable or not. The TAP recommends that a detailed due diligence process currently underway in which the Secretariat and DGG are discussing the management


---
**Page 116**
---

fee, with a clear focus on ensuring value for money, supported by PFCRD and a team of external expert advisors, including PWG.

184. Notwithstanding the above-mentioned concern, the independent TAP endorses this funding proposal for approval by the Board, acknowledging the high-risk-high-reward nature of the transaction.

185. In order to minimize the risks, the independent TAP further recommends that any approved funding be subject to the following conditions: (a) the inclusion of a condition precedent to the second disbursement to the AE under the funded activity agreement ("YAA"); and (b) the inclusion of the following covenants in the FAA:

(i) Immediate payment of all outstanding amounts due by the AE for the first disbursement under the FAA;

187. Delivery to GCF Secretariat by the AE at the final version of the programme operations manual, duly approved by the AE and including the content referred to in paragraphs (a) to (h) below, in a form and substance satisfactory to GCF.

(a) A final version of the GG Guaranty Policy in the form delivered as a condition precedent to the second disbursement under the FAA, reflecting the transaction selection process that sets out the GGC approach to providing guarantees, with reference to GGC's own procedures. The GG Guaranty Policy will include a more robust adaptation and mitigation section, including specific measures for addressing climate change impacts. This document shall be accompanied by a robust measurement, reporting and verification system that can serve for all transactions.

(b) A final version of the document that details the process and criteria for selecting the origination partners and the actions that GGC will take to build the origination partners' capacity.

(c) A clear description of the revised governance structure of GGC, with at least three levels of decision-making having clearly defined responsibilities. The description should cover members of both the Impact and Credit Committees. The description should also detail the roles of all parties involved in the transaction selection process, the climate advisors, the composition and role of the Investment Committee, the role of the Board, the GGC Secretariat, the DGG Impact Team, consultants, project appraisal/feasibility finding missions (DGG Impact Team, local experts, international experts, technical advisors, financial sector experts, legal matters etc.), and the investment decision makers (i.e. Credit Committee, GGC Board).

(e) An operations and business plan for conducting in each Host Country which has provided guarantees under the GG Guaranty Policy, including a list of potential project sponsors and the national private and public institutions that could potentially be involved in supporting the projects. The plan should also include an analysis of the climate finance market (e.g., size, structure, trends), as well as a description of the proposed financial structures, with a pipeline of GGC projects that will support each of the Host Countries' NDC goals.

(f) A final budget with the appropriate cost and management fees payable by GGC.


---
**Page 117**
---

(g) An engagement continuity plan for GGC (the “Management Continuity Plan”), including key person provisions, given the risk of personnel and/or institutional/company ownership structure changes over the 20-year period, with such plan to be approved by the holding company or its subsidiary as a shareholder of GGC (hereinafter referred to as “GGF” (“GGF Holdings”)), in its capacity as a shareholder of GGC, as a Shareholder Reserved Matter in accordance with the process set out in the GGC Shareholders’ Agreement and Management Agreement; and such plan shall include the design of business processes, formulation of policies on personnel and exit strategies and must be consistent with the GGC Shareholders’ Agreement and Management Agreement.

(h) A system of quality assurance will be established to ensure that all transactions under the GGC portfolio of Guarantees will be allocated to a specific group of countries: Host Countries that are below investment grade, as defined in the AAAA. The following shall be included:

(iii) Inclusion of the following covenants in the FAA:
(a) The Accredited Entity shall implement appropriate measures to manage and to deter, mitigate and/or correct any conflicts of interest, actual or perceived, between the Accredited Entity and any other party (including but not limited to any person who may be involved in other activities relating to GGC, including without limitation:

(i) A firewall policy for the management of information received from GGC, including but not limited to, sharing and discussing material non-public information received in connection with GGC and/or the programme; and

(ii) appropriate information barriers between the personnel of the Accredited Entity involved in carrying out the functions of the Accredited Entity under the AAAA and FAA.

(b) The Accredited Entity shall ensure that GGF implements appropriate measures to manage and to deter, mitigate and/or correct any conflicts of interest, actual or perceived, between the Accredited Entity and any other party (including but not limited to any person who may be involved in other activities relating to GGC, including without limitation, a firewall policy and governance procedures on improper use of, sharing and discussing material non-public information received from GGC.

(c) The Accredited Entity shall deliver to the GGF, once every two years following the effective date of the FAA, an updated Management Continuity Plan, for approval by GGF Holdings, as a Shareholder Reserved Matter in accordance with the process set out in the GGC Shareholders’ Agreement and Management Agreement; and

(d) The Accredited Entity shall deliver to the GGF, once every two years following the effective date of the FAA, an updated Management Continuity Plan, for approval by GGF Holdings, as a Shareholder Reserved Matter in accordance with the process set out in the GGC Shareholders’ Agreement and Management Agreement; and

(e) The Accredited Entity shall maintain a team of personnel at all relevant levels, each having the necessary knowledge and experience to act on such capacity, so as to enable the Accredited Entity to fulfill its obligations under the AAAA and FAA with respect to the implementation of the programme.

# Reply to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel assessment findings

## Proposal name: The Green Guarantee Company
## Accredited entity ("AE"): MUFG Bank, Ltd.

### Impact potential
The AE would like to thank the ITAP for its review of the impact potential of the Green Guarantee Company and its acknowledgment of the importance of developing the green bonds and green loan markets and the high demand for guarantees to scale up climate finance into these markets.
The AE also would like to thank the ITAP for its recognition of the Guarantee Policy, Transaction Selection Process, and the Transaction Selection Scorecard as the key tools for filtering opportunities to ensure impact potential is maximized. The AE welcomes the ITAP's suggestions to further improve the Guarantee Policy, the Transaction Selection Process and the Transaction Selection Scorecard.

### Paradigm shift potential
The AE thanks the ITAP for its review of the paradigm shift potential of the Green Guarantee Company, and it welcomes the suggestions to further strengthen the paradigm shift potential through refinements to the Guarantee Policy, Transaction Selection Process, Transaction Scorecard, governance arrangements, and the implementation of a management continuity plan.

### Sustainable development potential
The AE thanks the ITAP for its review of the sustainable development potential of the Green Guarantee Company, and it notes ITAP's suggestion that the Transaction Selection Process and the Transaction Scorecard should be refined to be able to screen for risks of maladaptation.

### Needs of the recipient
The AE thanks the ITAP for its review of the needs of the recipient for the Green Guarantee Company, and for its acknowledgment that the initial list of target countries has been selected to include examples of GCF priorities such as the least developed countries, small island developing states, and African entities. The AE welcomes ITAP's suggestion that an operations and business plan for each target country is developed.


---
**Page 119**
---

Country ownership

The AE thanks the ITAP for its review of country ownership for the Green Guarantee Company, and it welcomes ITAP’s suggestion to continue engagement with relevant public and private sector institutions in each target country to align purposes and to gain support in the origination of climate change transactions.

Efficiency and effectiveness

The AE thanks the ITAP for its review of the efficiency and effectiveness of the Green Guarantee Company, and it welcomes the suggestions to further strengthen the efficiency and effectiveness.

Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

The AE would like to thank the ITAP for its review and assessment of the Green Guarantee Company against the GCF investment criteria and notes with thanks its positive recommendation to the GCF Board to approve the Green Guarantee Company. The AE concurs with the sentiments of the ITAP that while the Green Guarantee Company’s innovative and ground-breaking approach is a significant step forward, it is also important to recognize and build on the significant and unparalleled potential of the Green Guarantee Company to leverage a market-based mechanism for the GCF to mobilize USD billions of climate finance from global credit and capital markets.

The AE is confident that the presence of Cardano Development, an established fund manager with a 15-year track record of investing in developing countries and USD 1 billion of capital under management, will provide the necessary expertise and experience to ensure the management continuity and good governance that ITAP rightly highlights as being essential for the success of the Green Guarantee Company. The AE is also confident from the fact that Cardano Development and the management team of the Development Guarantee Group have collectively successfully developed and scaled six guarantee companies in developing countries over a 15-year period, with a proven track record and as such ensures that GCF is working with the most experienced team possible on the Green Guarantee Company.

The AE confirms its agreement with the principles of the recommendations of the ITAP for development of the Green Guarantee Company and will work with the GCF Secretariat to integrate these principles into the operational and constitutional documentation of the Green Guarantee Company.

# Introduction

## The GREEN GUARANTEE COMPANY

Green Guarantee Company (GGC) is a guarantor for green and climate bonds and loans. GGC's mission is to help developing countries gain access to global capital for climate financing by transferring its investment-grade rating via a guarantee onto the developing country borrower. The guarantee on the bond or loan removes the credit risk of principal repayment, interest and payment delays having being GGC-rated as AAA, the issuer is unable to fulfill its obligations. Guaranteeing bonds and loans will help create enhanced climate change projects in developing countries, improving their sub-investment-grade credit ratings to investment grade so that they become eligible for investors from global capital markets to consider them for investment.

Green financial products and instruments will play a key role across all climate mitigation and climate adaptation strategies. These include green bonds and green loans that allocate the use of proceeds to eligible climate investments.

GGC's guarantees will credit enhance bonds and loans to allow the global capital market to invest in green and climate bonds and loans from developing countries - prioritizing decarbonization and low-carbon pathways across emerging markets. GGC’s objective is to use guarantees to drive climate mitigation projects which help develop a net-zero carbon economy and climate adaptation projects, while delivering environmental and social sustainability.

Rebuilding economies today to be green tomorrow can help avoid climate-borne financial and economic crises, protect today's investment in recovery, and produce lasting economic gains. A recent study by the World Bank (2021) found that investments in green infrastructure (representing 62 percent of the world’s population and 48 percent of global emissions) in selected sectors could generate $12.9 billion in investment opportunities, create 213 million cumulative new direct jobs and a billion-ton CO₂e reduction in GHG emissions annually between 2020-2030. GGC's portfolio covers 6 of these countries.

In addition to considering the positive impact associated with the financing being provided, GGC also recognizes the importance of ensuring sound gender and environmental and social (EES) practices to avoid or at least minimize potential financial and reputational liabilities. Consequently, the consideration of gender factors and safeguarding against environmental and social impacts is an integral part of our operational processes to ensure key risks and impacts are effectively managed throughout the transaction lifecycle.

## Background to the Assessment

The GGC fully adheres to the principle of country ownership, meaning that for each project that receives a guarantee from GGC (a Gender Assessment [Annex 8.1] and Action Plan [Annex 8.2]) will be in place and in alignment with the requirements set out by the GCF (GCF & UNWOMEN 2017), and the prevailing


---
**Page 125**
---

international ratified legal frameworks, regional and national gender policies, or other public policy for gender equality and women’s empowerment.

The project-based Gender Assessment and Action Plan will ensure that each project is designed and implemented in a manner fully aligned with other national development strategies that promote equal opportunities, poverty reduction and sustainable use of natural resources from a national, regional and sector-specific perspective. This will include analysis of the gender equality landscape and data relevant to the host country and in the targeted sector(s), including nationally determined Commitments (NDCs) and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).

This Annex serves as the foundation for the GGC’s responsiveness – through their guarantees – to supporting gender equality and women's empowerment, and to monitoring the links between gender equality, climate mitigation and adaptation, and environmental sustainability.

The Annex is informed by review of existing literature and secondary databases, as well as expert input and case study analyses, and is considered a living document that will be regularly updated. It comprises three sections:

1. Chapter 1: This introduction to the gender assessment, including general guiding principles.
2. Chapter 2: Narrative descriptions of if key gender issues and all considerations for each of the five results areas (such that the GGC will look to support projects in, building on the GGC’s vulnerabilities and capacities for change) and a high-level description of key gender considerations and constraints for each of these results areas. This chapter will also include an overview of how the Annex demonstrates the GGC's broad understanding of critical gender issues per results area and country, which will support investment decision making, inform the development of indicators for the Gender Action Plan (Annex 8.2), and be explored in greater depth once a project is under consideration by the GGC.
3. Chapter 3: A more detailed description of the GGC’s approach to practically ensuring projects are in alignment with the GGC’s approach to project-level gender mainstreaming.

This Gender Assessment will be used by the GGC, its AF, as well as project owners to ensure compliance with GGC's fiduciary principles and standards, environmental and social safeguards standards and Gender Policy.


| GGC is responsible for executing or carrying out the GCF-funded programmes and will review the gender action plans, assessment and Climate Impact Notes (CINs) of projects. |
| --- |
| The Accredited Entity is responsible for overseeing and monitoring that the GGC is consistent with the GGF fiduciary principles and standards, environmental and social safeguards standards, and Gender Policy. |

The GGC will ensure that all projects are in alignment with the GGC’s gender policy and requirements, in accordance with the relevant legal agreements (accreditation master agreements and funded activity agreements).

# 1.3 GENERAL PRINCIPLES

Prior to the issuance of a guarantee, a project owner should be aware of the following gender guidelines which are applicable during the investment process (complying relevant gender analysis or documents) as well as project implementation (for example reporting on progress):

- The project proposal considers key issues and requirements of gender sensitive design and implementation.
- The project is aligned with existing country policies and strategies and best practices (in the gender assessment, a policy, legal and regulatory review must be undertaken by the project owner).
- The project is aligned with national development strategies that promote equal opportunities, whether in the economic or social sectors.
- The project owner considers international legislation including discursive text issued by the Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW).
- The project owner will conduct stakeholder consultations and engagements in line with Annex 7.
- Sustainability is considered during the project design and complete the action plan.
- The project owner will be directed upon an event that requires to support the gender safeguards process that considers the project type, scale and context.
- The project owner should attempt to align the project with the Sustainable Development Goals and criteria to gender equality and women's empowerment (SDG 5) as well as relevant cross-cutting themes.
- The project owner should establish meaningful gender performance indicators for the project (gender indicators are established to measure and compare the situation of women and men over time). Gender indicators can be quantitative or qualitative. Quantitative indicators include numerical data, while qualitative indicators (based on how a woman's and man's experiences, attitudes, opinions and feelings), and these indicators must feed into the GGCA's overarching Gender Action Plan (Annex 8.2) so that the portfolio of project's gender-related progress can be aggregated and tracked.

By undertaking these guidelines, the project owner can build evidence of the gender gaps in markets and institutions and identify where project support is needed to address them. The project owner will continue to map gender constraints as well as opportunities that will seek to narrow gaps, lift constraints, support opportunities and feed into a more active and engaged dialogue with stakeholders on key levers for sustainable and inclusive change.

# Gender Assessments

The following section provides narrative descriptions of key gender issues and considerations per results area, building on the GGC’s vulnerabilities and capacities for change, as well as a high-level description of key gender considerations and commitments in each of the GGG’s 8 focus countries. The intention of this section is to provide the GGG with an understanding of the state of gender equality per country, which will support its decision-making, informing the implementation of actions for the Gender Action Plan (Annex B.2), and be explored in greater depth once a project is under consideration by the GGG.

The assessments were conducted in the absence of a project pipeline and is a living document that will be regularly updated to reflect key developments.


As has been described upfront in the Project Screening Proposal, the GGC has not yet identified specific projects nor finalized its decision on which projects, if any. Rather, they have committed to considering the existence of guarantees to climate projects in 8 select countries1, that fall within one or more of five results areas2. Until such time as the GGG is approached by a project investor / developer / owner with a specific project proposal, it is not possible – nor useful – to develop focussed and detailed gender analyses, assessments and action plans that the GGC would require as part of its Project Screening Proposal. It is however important for the GGC to possess and demonstrate a broad understanding of:

i. The nexus between gender and green bonds [elaborated further in Chapter 3];

ii. Gender-related vulnerabilities and capacities for change for each of the five results areas, that broadly pertain to the developing country that the GGG looks to support projects in;

iii. Each of the five results areas, as they relate to the specific project investor / developer / owner institutions and considerations (i.e., pressing issues that influence gender equality and women’s empowerment issues).

This information essentially provides a baseline of what the GGC hopes with regards to the select countries and results areas with respect to gender, and importantly, reflects the point of departure at which the GGG would require a project investor / developer / owner to provide a more detailed, context specific gender analysis, assessment and action plan that aligns with the GGG’s Gender Policy and related requirements. The findings from this document will be used by the GGC to inform its decision-making on whether or not it will use to identify potential gender-responsive interventions that should be considered as part of the project design process to maximise the gender benefits of a specific project. The framework summarises the most pressing gender challenges in each country relevant to climate change projects, and the sector-specific interventions that could be used by the GGG to address such challenges. This information thus serves as a basis for the GGC and the project investor / owner on the types of gender issues that a project in a specific country, within a specific sector, should seek to address.


1 Indonesia, Philippines, Brazil, Trinidad and Tobago, India, Rwanda, Laos and Gabon

2 Energy (e.g., power generation, transmission, distribution), transport, buildings, cities, infrastructure and applications; health, food and water security; infrastructure and the built environment

2.1 SECTOR/RESULTS AREA ASSESSMENTS

These assessments are desk-based and serve to elaborate on the GCF’s gender-related vulnerabilities and capacities for change. In doing so, this information describes the type of vulnerabilities that specific project-level gender assessments should expand on and investigate further, as well as the types of solutions (i.e., capacities for change) that should be integrated into project design and delivery processes.

2.1.1 ENERGY ACCESS AND POWER GENERATION

The GCF’s results area Energy Access and Power Generation is key to reducing reliance on fossil fuels, and thereby addressing greenhouse gas emission as well as driving economic growth. Projects under this area fall look to: generate energy from renewable sources such as wind, solar, geothermal, hydro, and sustainable bioenergy; efficiently and reliably transmit, distribute and store energy; and promote access to clean-energy technologies for households and businesses.

The on-grid energy sector, particularly in the developing world, has traditionally served urban and industrial hubs and underserved rural areas, thereby failing to meet the needs of the poor – especially women and women-headed households. The uneven distribution of energy and its distribution has resulted in gender inequalities in terms of access and use, which have had significant negative impacts. Therefore while it is universally accepted that access to energy is a catalyst for economic development, a driver for poverty alleviation, as well as a vehicle for women and girls empowerment, opportunities to increase access for women have not been prioritized.

The GCF’s results area Energy Access and Power Generation aims at promoting the transition towards sustainable and modern energy for all – linking closely with SDG6 on provision of equitable access to economic and natural resources. However, the developing world is still far from meeting SDG 7 in 2015. I. 2 billion people had no access to electricity and 85% of them were in non-Saharan Africa and Asia. In addition, one third of the global population has no access to modern energy services.

Significant progress has been made towards shifting power generation from fossils to renewable energy with the result that significant increases have been attained in 2016 to 2018, with as much as 37.6% of the total generation in OECD being from renewable sources (IEA 2020). Investment in RE has increased access to energy previously under served communities and improved the quality of electricity supply for existing services. Despite these advancements today over 700 million people worldwide still lack access to electricity.

The issues mentioned above necessitate energy sector work/transition to low or no carbon solutions, and to transform the economic, social and gendered aspects of the sector that have contributed to skewed and unequal development. Lack of electricity and energy services is directly correlated to poverty and related elements such as access to education and health. Further, energy poverty disproportionately affects women, making them more vulnerable to poverty, simply due to the fact that female-headed households are overrepresented among the people living in poverty (Caprara 1998). Energy access is therefore pivotal to economic empowerment. Recognizing the gender-energy poverty nexus in planning and implementing


---
**Page 129**
---

development programs can contribute to meaningful results that benefit women and address gender energy vulnerabilities such as those identified by GGC (along with capacities for change):

| Table 1: SGD Vulnerabilities and Capacities for Change | Energy and power generation |
| --- | --- |
| **Share of women and women-headed households without energy access** | **Roles of women in household power use** |
| **Impacts women are experiencing due to lack of energy access** | **Share of women in the energy labour market and decision-making** |
| **Policies and institutional framework to promote gender equality in energy & power generation** |  |

This discussion on vulnerabilities is generalized for GGC’s countries of focus. It refers to vulnerable women in poor households in rural areas as well as those in urban settings. Women have different energy needs compared to their male counterparts, which are often not met due to lack of access to energy services and decision-making in the home and in the community; and being limited sphere of influence to processes and resource allocation. In terms of energy: women’s roles are linked to energy provision and their sphere of influence being domestic (predominantly the kitchen). Yet decisions for household purchases and prioritization are the domain of men, who have greater access to income and resources. This results in fewer savings appliances. These gender roles translated into gender vulnerabilities that impact women negatively. These gender energy vulnerabilities broadly fall into three interwoven areas:

**Energy Poverty. Energy poverty is a result of poor incomes and is cyclic in nature such that, unless there are targeted interventions, will go on unabated for generations. Low incomes are associated with energy inefficient housing and appliances, lack of access to electricity, lack of modern lighting, and cooking fuel. People in energy poverty households perform more unpaid work, have less ability of time and tend to degrade the environment more than others due to their reliance on biomass for their dwelling construction and for cooking. Women and girls spend a huge portion of their time gathering and collecting firewood compared to those who have biogas or LPG. This has negative impacts on women’s health and education.**

**Education. Girls, who have access to electricity in their homes get a head start in life. Young girls are able to read at night; women are able to help their children after sunset and are generally safer from sexual violence. Children from homes with access to energy achieve much better in comparison to those without.**

**Health. Women and children suffer disproportionately from the lack of energy, with 3 million premature deaths linked are linked to household air pollution arising from cooking smoke (IEA 2020). In sub-Saharan Africa almost 490,000 premature deaths per year are related to household air pollution from the lack

## Empowerment through choices.

Women need to be able to make choices on what energy they need and how to use it. Having choices is a result of economic empowerment arising from better income and knowledge. Women believe that the provision of energy services would facilitate the ability of women to earn more through:

- Energy provision that allows for sufficient and affordable energy to meet both women's productive energy needs (cooking, food processing and water heating), and strategic energy needs (lighting, communication, etc.) in the community; women's enterprises; information for empowerment and engagement goals;
- Electricity supply which makes it possible for women to establish and run small businesses, in particular those that are rural-based.

## References

The GCF is committed to ensuring national policy frameworks and ensuring that energy generation is clean, affordable and appropriate to serve all, including rural and poor communities. It is critical that policies are introduced that address challenges or barriers that are faced by women entrepreneurs, and other vulnerable groups.

Currently, most those impacted by lack of energy services are in the rural areas, with the result that these current energy structures, policies and strategies are gender-blind and fail to meet the requirements or to address the constraints that rural women face. The structures focus on investment in electricity generation that largely target those in urban areas, leaving out the poor.


---
**Page 131**
---

3. Enabling income-generating opportunities: Effective energy programs/projects should aim to increase income in rural communities. This includes increasing farm yields, improving product quality and enabling the farmer to demand and receive fair prices for their produce. Energy is also a catalyst for creating entrepreneurial opportunities that women and youth can participate in, and it enables substitution of animate (human and animal) labour energy with mechanical technology to improve production and food security e.g. provision of irrigation water; improving food processing and storage for example with use of solar-powered drying.

4. Ensuring sustainability through local initiatives: To ensure the sustainability of off-grid SREI schemes in rural areas, there is a pressing need to promote the establishment of locally based rural agencies to provide maintenance services, including training on installation, operation, repair and replacement and supply of spare parts, and installing and repairing energy hardware. This thread can go further to capacitate small scale, self-supporting local technicians and businesses run by women and set in the rural areas.

Examples of how these types of interventions have been integrated into existing GCF energy and power projects:

P178: Desert to Power GS Safety Facility: The Green Sahel Project (which covers the countries of Burkina Faso, Chad, Mali, Mauritania and Niger) aims to increase energy access and security for women and men, and directly benefit 3.5 million people (out of which 1.75 million are women). More than 700, 000 women and men will also indirectly benefit from the project. To ensure effective gender mainstreaming, the project will address institutional and policy barriers following opportunities for women. Specifically, it will provide training to the staff of government agencies on banking and financial services, energy systems and service providers. Furthermore, the project will assist the five Sahel countries to develop gender-responsive regulatory frameworks.

FP168: Leveraging Energy Access Finance (LEAF) Framework: This program (covering Nigeria, Kenya, Ghana, Gueve, Ethiopia and Tunisia) seeks to unlock local currency debt capital to scale up decentralized and distributed renewable energy in the African continent. LEAF aims to provide access to clean and reliable energy services for 10 million people by 2030 through a combination of investments in Decentralized Renewable Energy companies to deploy Solar Home Systems (SHS), Green Mini Grids (GMG) and Captive power solutions. The Program will facilitate access to credit for women for SHS, GMG and captive power solutions, and is expected to create 15,000 jobs for women during the scale-up of DRE companies.

2.1.2 LOW EMISSION TRANSPORT

The GCF’s Transport results area focuses on increasing access to low-emission transport by supporting low- and zero-emission public and private transport systems. Such schemes typically fall under city or country-wide cross-cutting GCF projects, such as Green Cities or Resident Urban Systems, with the inclusion of climate resilient connectivity options. Such projects are aligned with SDG 11 “Sustainable Cities and Communities”.


---
**Page 132**
---

which includes a specific target (11.2) that commits countries to providing "access to safe, affordable, accessible and sustainable transport systems for all" in improving road safety, notably by expanding public transport, with special attention to the needs of those in vulnerable situations, women, children, persons with disabilities and older persons". In doing so, this target enables the attainment of SDG 5's (Gender Equality) commitments to support women in receiving equal rights to economic resources. It is within this context that the GCF has defined specific vulnerabilities and capacities for change (Error! Reference source not found.).

Towards creating meaningful change for women and girls who rely on transport for economic, educational, domestic and recreational activities, GGC will seek to ensure that any low-emission transport projects that it supports, contribute to unlocking and enabling these capacities for change.

| Vulnerabilities | Capacities for Change |
| --- | --- |
| - Differences among urban or public transport, share of women without access to public transport | - Share of the community/family with regard to transport patterns |
| - Impacts women and men are experiencing due to lack of/ poor quality of transport. | - Share of women and men in the transport labour market |
|  | - Policies and institutional framework to promote gender equality in transport sector |

With transport being one of the leading contributors of GHG emissions, there has been a global push to promote investment in sustainable systems that support a move away from private motorized focus on low emission public transport, walking and cycling (World Bank 2021). As part of many national responses to build back better in the wake of COVID-19, many countries have seen this as an opportunity time to make shifts in transport systems. However, these shifts are often not gender-sensitive and can exacerbate existing inequalities within the transport sector; "entrenching transport-disadvantages, poverty and social exclusion" where women's needs and rights have historically been largely overlooked, and women are significantly underrepresented in transport investment and decision making (Jennings 2020). This undermines progress against SDG 5 and SDG 11, amongst others.

Therefore, the impact and sustainability of shifts and improvements in transport systems will largely depend on the degree to which women's other vulnerable groups' needs are taken into consideration, as a basis for influencing and informing investment and design decisions. As a starting point, it is important to consider existing differences in transport patterns and travel behaviour of men and women, and how existing systems respond to these differences.

Differences in travel behavior: Women's travel is considered 'multi-purpose' due to the gender-based division of work in most households, which means that women require transport for their more varied obligations - ranging from formal and informal employment, to domestic tasks and caregiving (i.e.


---
**Page 133**
---

GCF_GFC_Gender Assessment_Annex_8_14.10.2022_clean__Final.docx

Draft | **14**

accompanying children and elderly relatives to school, clinics, etc.) (International Transport Forum 2019) (Jennings 2020). While social characteristics (such as income status, age, number of dependents, etc.) influence women's transport decisions, their travel typically comprises shorter distances, with more frequent multi-leg trips that often take place during off-peak hours. Trips are also often multi-modal to allow for more flexibility (International Transport Forum 2019). This means, that despite women predominantly having lower access and affordability of transport services than men, they make more trips per day, which is important for health, food and care responsibilities – in addition to the transport they require for income-generating activities (Jennings 2020).

Gender CC also identifies a set of gender impacts on human health in the transport sector, whereby women face greater risks and challenges than men. These include: the 'women's vulnerability of transport heavy loads over long distances; inadequate road safety legislation; harassment and public transportation violence against women; and lack of access to safe walking areas when traveling in cities' (Jennings 2020). A n.d. study is important of whether post-COVID-19 transportation systems in sub-Saharan Africa were serving the needs of women highlighted similar physical risk factors, which are often compounded by cultural restrictions that limited the way in which women move (time away from home, locations they may travel to, mode of transport, need for überhaupt).

Access to opportunities. Gendered mobility barriers hinder women's access to income-generating opportunities – a concern that was highlighted in 2017 ILO report, which identified 'lack of transport as the greatest challenge to the female labour force'. This is a clear contributing factor to the fact that, in 2021, only 48% of women participated in the formal workforce globally, compared with 75% of men (World Bank 2021). Female workers are more likely than male workers to use transport costs, which often limit their access to income opportunities and services and markets (Jennings 2020).

Underrepresentation of women in the transport sector. Despite the above findings, mobility plans rarely consider the safety, security, comfort, accessibility and affordability needs of women and girls (UNEP 2020). This is partly linked to the underrepresentation of women in the transport sector globally, which has led to the marginalization of their needs and interests in planning processes. In many countries, women are predominantly employed into lower paid/low status jobs with few, if any, opportunities for career development. One of the biggest barriers to women's entry into the transport labour force is the high rate of violence against transport workers; this affects the ability to encourage women to quality and work in the sector (Saunders 2019).

Research has shown that women are vital to a low-carbon economy transition, which includes more integrated mobility sector. Improving gender equality and safety in transport will lead to higher levels of uptake of low emission public transport, biking and walking (Saunders 2019). Achieving this requires a range of interventions, from policy reforms to targeted and meaningful engagement with women, and capacity building – both for transport agencies and operators, and those working within the sector. These capacities for change will be integrated as appropriate into programs that GCF seeks to support (Saunders 2019).


---
**Page 134**
---

1. Development of gender-informed transport policies, strategies and regulations, that draw on up-to-date sex and gender analyses and participatory planning (i.e.: gender inclusive consultations) with both female and male beneficiaries.
2. On-going routine analysis, and evaluation and reporting, of gender and transport issues during transport planning, implementation and operations to identify physical, economic and social risk factors that limit affordability and access of women and girls to public transport services. This includes monitoring user patterns, to ensure services respond to specific mobility needs of women and girls, who utilise transport to access health services, education and economic opportunities.
3. Awareness-raising and capacity-building for transport agencies and service operators to ensure systems are designed with activity management and respond to gender issues both internally (with employees) and externally (with users).

Examples of how these types of interventions have been integrated into existing GCF Transport projects are described below:

FP156: Light Rail Transit for the Greater Metropolitan Area (GAM). To address the significant underrepresentation of women in Costa Rica’s transport sector, the project has committed to generating direct and indirect jobs for women through (i) the filling and provision of spaces for women entrepreneurs to be part of the business model; (ii) the creation of a gender equality committee at the company level; and (iii) the attraction of women to join the train workforce by committing to supporting equal opportunities, and protect female employees through the development of protocols to prevent sexual harassment in the workplace.

FP503: Costa Rica BRT System. Towards developing a zero-emissions bus rapid transit (BRT) system that is safer and accessible to all, this project has committed to conducting periodic ridership surveys to track changes in (i) race and diversity of riders; and (ii) their satisfaction level, the results of which will feed into decision-making regarding routes, pricing, peak and off-peak hours. An awareness raising program will also be rolled out addressing issues of harassment, violence, bullying, theft, and other security concerns, with all employees trained to recognize and report incidents and other crimes.

2.1.3 BUILDINGS, CITIES, INDUSTRIES AND APPLIANCES

Cities are hubs for ideas that can help them meet their high carbon footprints, offer opportunities for addressing climate change with potential to achieve high positive impact overall. Thus, the GCF has identified Buildings, cities and industries as one of the eight key result areas. This result area by its nature is cross-cutting and covers a wide range of activities in the following four thematic areas: 1) Energy efficiency; 2) Infrastructure and built environment; and 3) Health Food and water security. The “Buildings, Cities, Industries and Appliances” result area focuses on the adoption of better technologies, energy efficiency, efficient energy designs and introduction of incentives that change behavior to help reduce energy use.

The result area also responds to a number of sustainable development goals, in particular SDG 11 "Sustainable Cities and Communities", SDG 7.3 “Energy Efficiency”, SDG 9 “Industry, Innovation and

## Key Determinants of Vulnerability in Urban Areas

### Housing, Shelter and Health Risks
Housing provision for cities in the developing world is insufficient and inadequate, with better housing and living spaces being accessed by the more affluent members of the society. 40% of the poorest households in urban areas are headed by women (UNDESA 2021), meaning a large proportion of women live in substandard or informal settlements. These have various shelter deprivations that include energy inefficient appliances or no appliances at all, poor ventilation, and lack of or unreliable water and sanitation supplies.

## More inclusive and efficient city decision-making

Gender equality in local, regional and national decision making improves the ability of women to influence policies and politics. Including women in the decision-making process leads to better informed decisions on how to design services and use resources more efficiently and equitably. This is particularly important for the built environment based on gender roles, can contribute significantly to the capacity to adapt to climate change.

## Inclusive transport systems

As described under the Transport Results Atlas, city transport should not only cater for example, a bus system that links neighbourhoods to economic centres (thereby addressing men's concerns) but also include services which allow better connections from home to the market or childcare centres. Transport networks must also be designed with women in mind and provide access to residential areas, for use by women who provide domestic services.

## Improving access and affordability of appliances and green buildings

Improving access to affordable and energy-efficient appliances can have a significant impact on the level of use of energy efficient and affordable appliances by women. It has been shown that use of appliances enables women to achieve more economic goals, reduce use of dirty energy and saves time. This also includes Upgrading water heating and cooling appliances and retrofitting with energy efficient technologies.

## Improving access to clean cooking fuels and improved cook stoves

The provision of clean cooking fuels and improved cook stoves, lighting, the siting of markets and latrines and the provision of safe transport, including the training of drivers and conductors, are all areas that could contribute to greater safety for all - and in particular women, children and other vulnerable groups.

## The following GCF projects aim to reduce emissions from buildings, cities, industries and appliances by supporting policies, standards and technologies that reduce the need for energy

FP068_Green City Facility. This program aims to enable cities to transition to low-carbon, climate-resilient urban development. In doing so, the Facility will help the target cities to mainstream gender to ensure that equal opportunities are afforded to women through employment and participation in decision-making forums to ensure that their needs and concerns are addressed when services are being rolled out. Through training it will build the capacity of women to participate in policy dialogue activities and promote gender equality in access to services and employment through information sharing.

FP103 Promotion of Climate-Friendly Cooking: Kenya and Senegal. This project aims to accelerate the growth of Improved Cook stoves (ICS) markets in Kenya and Senegal and significantly increase the level and quality of ICS production and sales, in rural areas and poor urban areas. The project is estimated to directly benefit 11.25 million people, mostly women as consumers and users of cook stoves. The project will also create income generation opportunities from manufacturing of cook stoves and ensure the involvement of

# HEALTH, FOOD AND WATER SECURITY

With the population projected to reach 9.7 billion by 2050, there is a pressing need to increase food production by 50% (Population Division, UN 2020). This global food security crisis requires significant scaling up in both the quantity and quality of food produced. The crisis also requires reducing the sector’s environmental footprint while reducing the context of climate change. Recognising this, the GCF’s Health, Food and Water Security results area seeks to support resilient, low emissions agricultural and food systems towards a three-fold paradigm shift: 1) Promoting sustainable land management; 2) Improving access to nutritious foods for all people; and 3) Reconfiguring food systems. In doing so, GCF projects under this results area directly serve to support several critical targets under SDG 2: End hunger, achieve food security and improved nutrition and promote sustainable agriculture.

Specifically:
- **SDG 2.1:** "end hunger and ensure access by all people, in particular the poor and people in vulnerable situations, including infants, children, adolescents, adults, and older persons"
- **SDG 2.2:** "end all forms of malnutrition, achieving, by 2025, the internationally agreed targets on stunting and wasting in children under 5 years old, and addressing the nutritional needs of adolescent girls, pregnant and lactating women and other persons"
- **SDG 2.3:** "double the agricultural productivity and income of small-scale food producers, in particular women, indigenous peoples, family farmers, pastoralists and fishers", including through secure and equal access to land, other productive resources and inputs, knowledge, financial services, markets and opportunities for value addition and non-farm employment"

These three targets are all directly linked to SDG 5 on Gender Equality, and collectively point to the fundamental role that women play in supporting a shift in global agricultural practices. However, while women make up more than half of the world’s food producers, they do not get full access to the resources and services that farmers need to sustainably operate (IFAD 2020). This is due to a variety of challenges and vulnerabilities that they face, which collectively place women – and their dependents – at greater risk from food insecurity, food security or health-related hazards as defined by the GCF. See Emori Reference for more details.

Whilst these vulnerabilities manifest differently based on country-specific contexts, the following overarching challenges prevail in many developing countries:

### Differences in roles and responsibilities

In addition to making up a significant component of the global agricultural workforce, women spend more time than men to take part on-time, seasonal or low-wage work – women are also responsible for the vast majority of unpaid labour: house chores, cooking, livestock rearing, childcare and caring for the elderly (S. Huyer et al 2021). In bearing these responsibilities, women are often more likely than men to spend their money on health-related items for their families – such that, when women’s income is higher in a household, children have better nutrition over household budgets; communities are generally healthier and more resilient (Rainforest Alliance 2020).

### Impacts of climate change

Women's on-going domestic and increased residual responsibilities are further compounded by the increasing prevalent impacts that climate change is having on water and food security (UN 2019). Women are significantly more vulnerable than men to the effects of climate change due to their strong reliance on natural resources for their livelihoods. Climate change also increases the time required by women and girls to collect water, multiplication and ill health result from food and water insecurity and poor water quality; and rates of gender-based violence increase as households become more resource constrained (IFAD 2020) (IUCN 2020). Women also tend to be poorer, meaning their options to act in times of crisis are limited.

### The agriculture sector

Where the poorest rural women overwhelmingly work is hardest hit by droughts, floods and heatwaves (IFAD 2020) and without access to critical resources (described below), women lack the adaptive capacity to recover from; and respond to such shocks and stressors.


#### Access to resources (land, finances, skills and technology)

Women own substantially less land – including agricultural land (15%, versus 89%) than men; and even in instances where country’s recognize women’s ownership of land, their rights are not guaranteed (IFAD 2020) (UNWOMEN 2013). Increasing women's tenure security will increase their production capacity, and can also be used as collateral to access finance. However, challenges in accessing such forms of collateral are often coupled with limited financial products adapted to women’s needs; meaning access to credit is a significant barrier for women (S. Huyer et al 2021). Women also lack access to efficient, resilient, low carbon systems and technologies (S. Huyer et al 2021). FAO research has shown that giving women equal access to natural and financial resources; education and land leads to increased farm productivity (by as much as 30%) and improvements in family nutrition.

It is therefore critical that agricultural projects focused on enhancing health, food and water security outcomes be carried out with a gender perspective and analysis, as this is key to unlocking productivity, improving food and nutrition security, reducing poverty; and enhancing resilience (FAO, IFAD & World Bank 2015). Such approaches for doing so include (UNWOMEN 2021):


---
**Page 140**
---

1. Unlocking bottlenecks for women’s participation in rural value chains, by supporting policy reforms and identifying measures that improve women’s land rights, establishing and capacitating women cooperatives and women-owned micro, small and medium enterprises (MSMEs), and providing appropriate financial and insurance products.
2. Climate smart agriculture skills upfiment and technology transfer to support women farmers and empower them with the necessary knowledge and practices to CSA approaches through the introduction of climate resilient agricultural technologies and the incorporation of renewable and energy-efficient technologies across value chains.
3. Access to climate information and early warning systems, in a timely manner and in appropriate formats (e.g., SMS, text messages, use of maps, use of media / telephone networks etc.) so that women farmers are able to make decisions and implement disaster preparedness measures in advance of adverse events such as to protect produce, homesteads, and farming infrastructure / equipment.

Examples of how these types of interventions have been integrated into existing Health, Food and Water Security projects are described below:

FP175: Enhancing community resilience and water security in the Upper Athi River Catchment Area, Kenya. In establishing a modernized hydro-meteorological observation, monitoring, and testing system for the catchment area, women farmers will be provided with training on how to use the system and will be engaged in the installation and rehabilitation processes, and that the percentage level of women’s access to and utilization of the network systems, be closely monitored to ensure equitable access.

FP179: Tanzania Agriculture Climate Adaptation Technology Deployment Programme. The project has set ambitious targets with respect to providing targeted financial solutions and products (loans, guarantees and insurance) for smallholder farmers in order to increase their resilience to climate change impacts and climate change adaptation technologies and solutions. This includes the development of women-dedicated credit line options guarantee solutions that integrate women-specific interests. The project will also seek to ensure that 60% of local financial institutions financing agriculture activities are sensitized to lending and guarantee provisions for women farmers.

2.1.5 INFRASTRUCTURE AND THE BUILT ENVIRONMENT

Developing countries are exposed to worsening climate related hazards, storms, tropical cyclones, floods, sea level rises and droughts as well as secondary hazards such as flash flooding and landslips. The majority of human and direct economic losses in developing countries are caused by damage to the built environment (Kachura Ginge et al 2014). Insofar as infrastructure is concerned, the damage caused by extreme weather events results in huge sums of money being devoted to repairs, damage control, rubble clearing, resettlement of displaced people and humanitarian assistance; inevitably pushing governments into debt crises, bankrupting businesses and impoverishing individuals. A significant number are not able to recover without external

## Slow evacuations causing fatalities.

Poor and vulnerable households (women headed households, including the elderly and those living with disabilities) tend to evacuate much slower than others in times of extreme events thus suffering more fatalities (S. Kotegoda 2011). Some fail to get early warnings due to lack of communication gateways or time to read and listen to news.

Loss of homes due to poorly constructed buildings. Once more, women headed households and the poor are affected most as their structures are shabby constructed and not robust to withstand extreme weather conditions. This is a major cause of food insecurity and malnutrition in vulnerable communities.

Food Insecurity and famine. Women and specifically, women-headed households, rely on subsistence agriculture to produce their food needs. They generally practice rain-fed agriculture in low-lying lands or along river banks. These fields are prone to flooding events and when that occurs, the poor fail to supply their families with food and have to resort to humanitarian aid. Famine is common in African countries as a result of prolonged drought periods. famine is a slow killer and robs women of her dignity.

Disruption of local security and safety nets. Climate-related disasters such as flood, drought or famine may disrupt local security safety nets, leaving women and children undocumented, displaced or orphaned due to the ensuing loss of livelihoods and assets. Women are more vulnerable in these situations. Lack of food and much left in built area and the little that exists can dissipate as people seek emergency shelters during displacement.

Unsafe shelters or camps. After a natural disaster, economic and dignity challenges may lead women who are in charge of households and livelihood to seek temporary shelter and amenable living conditions in acute insecurity contexts, making their potential targets for exploitation and human trafficking. Disasters that lead to increased physical, social and economic insecurity, and affect women and children, are among some of the push factors that give rise to human trafficking.

Responses to these vulnerabilities should seek to provide climate resilient infrastructure solutions that specifically look to strengthen women’s resilience to external shocks. Approaches to supporting women’s knowledge and skills in disaster risk reduction and building resilience have proven to be effective in minimizing women's vulnerabilities (Katharina Grigat et al., 2019). Such interventions include the following:

* Coordination of infrastructure development policies, ensuring climate and gender mainstreaming.
* Due to the multi-sectoral nature of the built environment, it is critical that all relevant sectors are part of the policy development process towards ensuring policies are harmonized. Therefore, a systematic coordination approach should be adopted in order to ensure that all stakeholders are aware of how climate and gender considerations can be crucial in contributing towards cohesive resilience at national and subnational scales.
* Development of inclusive building codes and standards. Construction industries in the developing world lack adequate regulations, and levels of regulation are also variable. Hence, in some cases, building standards are weak or not adhered to. These structures cannot withstand extreme events. For communities that live in informal settlements including slums, there are no building standards that are enforced. Slums are frequently wiped out during disaster events. Countries need to assess and revise their building codes and standards to ensure both climate proofing and gender mainstreaming is
